Viscoelastic Cahn–Hilliard models for tumour growth


Abstract
We introduce a new phase field model for tumour growth where viscoelastic effects are taken into account. The model is derived from basic thermodynamical principles and consists of a convected Cahn–Hilliard equation with source terms for the tumour cells and a convected reaction-diffusion equation with boundary supply for the nutrient. Chemotactic terms, which are essential for the invasive behaviour of tumours, are taken into account. The model is completed by a viscoelastic system constisting of the Navier–Stokes equation for the hydrodynamic quantities, and a general constitutive equation with stress relaxation for the left Cauchy–Green tensor associated with the elastic part of the total mechanical response of the viscoelastic material. For a specific choice of the elastic energy density and with an additional dissipative term accounting for stress diffusion, we prove existence of global-in-time weak solutions of the viscoelastic model for tumour growth in two space dimensions d = 2 d=2 by the passage to the limit in a fully-discrete finite element scheme where a CFL condition, i.e. Δ ​ t ≤ C ​ h 2 \Delta t\leq Ch^{2} , is required. Moreover, in arbitrary dimensions d ∈ { 2 , 3 } d\in\{2,3\} , we show stability and existence of solutions for the fully-discrete finite element scheme, where positive definiteness of the discrete Cauchy–Green tensor is proved with a regularization technique that was first introduced by Barrett and Boyaval [ 6 ] . After that, we improve the regularity results in arbitrary dimensions d ∈ { 2 , 3 } d\in\{2,3\} and in two dimensions d = 2 d=2 , where a CFL condition is required. Then, in two dimensions d = 2 d=2 , we pass to the limit in the discretization parameters and show that subsequences of discrete solutions converge to a global-in-time weak solution. Finally, we present numerical results in two dimensions d = 2 d=2 . Keywords: Mathematical modelling, viscoelasticity, tumour growth, finite element method. MSC Classification:

Fakultät für Mathematik, Universität Regensburg, 93053 Regensburg, Germany

Harald.Garcke@ur.de

Balázs Kovács

Fakultät für Mathematik, Universität Regensburg, 93053 Regensburg, Germany

Balazs.Kovacs@ur.de

Fakultät für Mathematik, Universität Regensburg, 93053 Regensburg, Germany

Dennis.Trautwein@ur.de

1
Introduction

In the past few years, the study of mathematical models for tumour growth has become a popular topic of research.
Even though many biological processes with regard to tissue growth are very complicated and still not fully understood, mathematical models try to give an insight into the qualitative behaviour of the most significant processes.
Yet, the main difficulty is to choose the model in a way such that the individual properties of the respective biological material are described as good as possible.

Here, material laws play a decisive role and several different approaches have been proposed in the literature.
Detailed comparisons with
in vivo
experiments indicate that neglecting the elastic effects completely would be too restrictive, as mechanical stresses have a noticeable impact on the growth behaviour
[
42
]
. Hence, living tissues are sometimes modelled as an elastic solid where the behaviour is described with linear or nonlinear elasticity.
Moreover, there are models that refer to very short time scales for stress relaxation and thus propose viscous approaches, as they allow to consider the random and directional movement of the cells qualitatively, which is a well-known behaviour of tumour cells
[
52
]
.
On the other hand, the behaviour of tumour cells within the extracellular matrix resembles granular material for which usually Darcy’s law is prescribed
[
4
]
.

A popular ansatz in the literature is to combine multiple material laws at once.
For example, Brinkman’s law is used to describe material featuring properties of granular material and viscous fluids
[
23
]
.
To account for viscous and elastic properties, viscoelastic approaches are very helpful and they are mostly studied in the context of polymeric fluids
[
6
,
9
,
46
]
. Although there exist viscoelastic models for tumour growth
[
4
,
15
,
55
]
, there is still a huge gap in the literature concerning the derivation and mathematical analysis, especially for phase field approaches.
A Cahn–Hilliard model coupled to viscoelasticity with a Neo-Hookean finite elasticity, which is different to the one in the present paper, has been derived and analyzed in
[
2
]
.

The goal of this work is to introduce and study a new mathematical model for tumour growth where viscoelasticity is taken into account.
The general mathematical model of our interest is given by the following nonlinear system of partial differential equations.

Problem
:

Find
\varphi,\mu,\sigma,p:\Omega\times(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}:\Omega\times(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{B}:\Omega\times(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that in
\Omega\times(0,T)
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi+\operatorname{div}\left({\varphi\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({m(\varphi)\nabla\mu}\right)+\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\mathbb{B}),

(1.1a)

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=A\psi^{\prime}(\varphi)-B\Delta\varphi+N_{,\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma)+W_{,\varphi}(\varphi,\mathbb{B}),

(1.1b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma+\operatorname{div}\left({\sigma\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({n(\varphi)\nabla N_{,\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)}\right)-\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\mu,\sigma),

(1.1c)

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\mathbb{B}),

(1.1d)

\displaystyle\varrho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p,\mathbb{B})}\right)+\big(\mu-W_{,\varphi}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\big)\nabla\varphi+N_{,\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)\nabla\sigma,

(1.1e)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T},

(1.1f)

where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\{2,3\}
, is a bounded domain and
T>0
is a fixed time. Here, the viscoelastic stress tensor is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p,\mathbb{B})\coloneqq\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{visc}}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p)+\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B}),

(1.2)

where the viscous and the elastic parts of the stress tensor are defined as

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{visc}}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\eta(\varphi)\big(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}\big)+\lambda(\varphi)\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\mathbb{I}-p\mathbb{I},

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2W_{,\mathbb{B}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\mathbb{B}.

(1.4)

The above system is composed of a convected Cahn–Hilliard system (
1.1a
)–(
1.1b
) for the order parameter
\varphi\in[-1,1]
denoting the difference of volume fractions, with
\{\varphi=1\}
representing the unmixed tumour tissue and
\{\varphi=-1\}
representing the surrounding healthy tissue, and the chemical potential
\mu
related to the phase field variable
\varphi
. This system is coupled to a convected parabolic diffusion equation (
1.1c
) where
\sigma
denotes the concentration of an unknown species serving as a nutrient for the tumour.
We include hydrodynamic effects through the viscoelastic system (
1.1d
)–(
1.1e
) with constant mass density
\varrho
for the volume-averaged velocity
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
, the pressure
p
and the viscoelastic stress tensor
\mathbb{T}
. Here,
\mathbb{B}
denotes the left Cauchy–Green tensor associated with the elastic part of the total mechanical response of the viscoelastic fluid and it is given by the constitutive equation (
1.1f
) of Oldroyd-B type
[
51
]
, but other constitutive equations for
\mathbb{B}
are also possible, e.g., a constitutive equation of Giesekus type
[
33
]
, i.e.

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{B}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(1.5)

By
N_{,\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma)
and
N_{,\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)
, we denote the variations of a general nutrient energy density
N(\varphi,\sigma)
with respect to
\varphi
and
\sigma
, respectively. Similarly, by
W_{,\varphi}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})
and
W_{,\mathbb{B}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})
, we denote the variations of a general elastic energy density
W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})
with respect to
\varphi
and
\mathbb{B}
. These energies will be specified later.

The positive constants
A,B
usually have the form
A=\frac{\beta}{\epsilon}
and
B=\beta\epsilon
, where
\epsilon
is proportional to the thickness of the diffuse interface and
\beta
represents the surface tension. By
m(\cdot)
and
n(\cdot)
, we denote the non-negative mobilities for the order parameter
\varphi
and the nutrient
\sigma
, respectively, and
\psi(\cdot)
is a non-negative potential with two equal minima at
\pm 1
.
Biological effects like proliferation, apoptosis and nutrient consumption are taken into account through the source and sink terms
\Gamma_{\varphi}
and
\Gamma_{\sigma}
. Moreover,
\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
denotes a source for the velocity divergence and is often related to
\Gamma_{\varphi}
.
The non-negative functions
\eta(\cdot)
and
\lambda(\cdot)
denote the shear and bulk viscosities, respectively. The non-negative function
\tau(\cdot)
is the viscoelastic relaxation time accounting for dissipation.

We now present the outline of this work. We end Section
1
by introducing our notation.
Then, in Section
2
, we present the derivation of the general viscoelastic model for tumour growth
using basic thermodynamical principles and we give several examples of constitutive laws. Moreover, we highlight further important aspects of modelling like a dissipation law for a general energy of the system, reformulations of the pressure leading to variants of the velocity equation (
1.1e
), and initial and boundary conditions. We also give relevant examples for the source functions
\Gamma_{\varphi},\Gamma_{\sigma},\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
.
Further, we specify the nutrient energy density
N(\varphi,\sigma)
and the elastic energy density
W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})
.
Besides, we handle the case of possible source or sink terms due to growth in the equation for
\mathbb{B}
, and we present several limit cases of our model
which were introduced for other models in the literature.

In Section
3
, we consider a special variant of the problem
which is additionally regularized with a dissipative term
\alpha\Delta\mathbb{B}
in the Oldroyd-B equation, and the regularized problem is denoted by
. This regularization improves the mathematical properties of the governing equations while it has a minor impact on the dynamical behaviour of the model, supposed that the viscoelastic diffusion constant
\alpha
is small.
For
, we give the definition of weak solutions and provide an existence result in two spatial dimensions in Section
3.1
. To highlight the difficulties and to better understand the techniques in the proof of the existence result, we present the derivation of formal
a priori
estimates in Section
3.2
, the need for the restriction to two dimensions in Section
3.3
and a regularization strategy from Barrett and Boyaval
[
6
]
in Section
3.4
which is needed to show positive definiteness of the Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}
.
The existence result itself will be proved in Section
4
by the limit passing in a fully-discrete finite element scheme in two dimensions where a CFL condition, i.e.
\Delta t\leq Ch^{2}
, is required, whereby Section
4
is organized as follows. First, a regularized fully-discrete finite element scheme is introduced in arbitrary dimensions
d\in\{2,3\}
, where the regularization strategy from Barrett and Boyaval
[
6
]
is applied on the fully-discrete level. For the regularized discrete scheme, stability and existence in arbitrary dimensions
d\in\{2,3\}
are shown in Sections
4.3
and
4.4
, respectively. Then, in Section
4.5
, the regularization parameter is sent to zero which guarantees that the discrete Cauchy–Green tensor is positive definite. After that, the regularity of the discrete solutions is improved in arbitrary dimensions
d\in\{2,3\}
in Section
4.6
and in also in two dimensions
d=2
in Section
4.7
, where a CFL condition, i.e.
\Delta t\leq Ch^{2}
, is needed. Then, Section
4.8
is devoted to the limit process
(\Delta t,h)\to(0,0)
in two space dimensions, where existence of global-in-time weak solutions is provided by converging subsequences of the discrete solutions. Finally, in Section
5
, we present numerical results for the fully-discrete tumour model from Section
4
in two spatial dimensions.

1.1
Notation

In this work, vector or matrix valued quantities are represented with a bold or blackboard bold font, respectively.
For
d\in\{2,3\}
, we define the scalar product of two vectors
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}\coloneqq\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
, and the scalar product of two matrices
\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
by
\mathbb{A}:\mathbb{B}\coloneqq\trace(\mathbb{A}^{T}\mathbb{B})=\trace(\mathbb{B}^{T}\mathbb{A})
, where
\trace(\mathbb{A})
denotes the trace of a matrix
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
Moreover,
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{S}}
and
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{SPD}}
are the sets of symmetric
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and symmetric positive definite
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
matrices, respectively.
For a vector or matrix valued quantity, we denote the induced norm by
\left\lvert{\cdot}\right\rvert
, and for a scalar quantity, we denote by
\left\lvert{\cdot}\right\rvert
the Euclidean norm.
For a real Banach space
X
, we denote by
\|{\cdot}\|_{X}
its norm, by
X^{\prime}
its dual space, and by
\left<{\cdot,\cdot}\right>_{X}
the duality pairing between
X
and
X^{\prime}
.
For
p\in[1,\infty]
, an integer
m\geq 0
and a bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\{2,3\}
, we use the standard notation from, e.g.,
[
3
]
, and we write
L^{p}\coloneqq L^{p}(\Omega)
,
W^{m,p}\coloneqq W^{m,p}(\Omega)
and
H^{m}\coloneqq H^{m}(\Omega)\coloneqq W^{m,2}(\Omega)
, where
W^{0,p}\coloneqq L^{p}
in the case
m=0
. We also define
L^{2}_{0}\coloneqq L^{2}_{0}(\Omega)\coloneqq\{q\in L^{2}\mid\int_{\Omega}q\,{\mathrm{d}}x=0\}
and
H^{1}_{0}\coloneqq H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\coloneqq\{q\in H^{1}\mid q|_{\partial\Omega}=0
a.e. on
\partial\Omega\}
, where
q|_{\partial\Omega}
should be interpreted in the sense of the trace theorem.
We sometimes use the same notation for vector valued or matrix valued spaces.
For instance,
L^{p}
can mean
L^{p}(\Omega)
,
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
or
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
, which of course depends on the context.
Moreover, we define
\mathbf{H}\coloneqq\left\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\right)=0\text{ a.e.\penalty\ in }\Omega,\ \boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega\right\}
and
\mathbf{V}\coloneqq\left\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\right)=0\text{ a.e.\penalty\ in }\Omega\right\}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}
denotes the outer unit normal on
\partial\Omega
.
The norms and seminorms of the Sobolev spaces are denoted by
\|{\cdot}\|_{W^{m,p}}
and
\left\lvert{\cdot}\right\rvert_{W^{m,p}}
, respectively, and similarly for the spaces
L^{p}
and
H^{m}
.
We denote the inner product of the spaces
L^{2}
and
L^{2}({\partial\Omega})
by
\left({\cdot,\cdot}\right)_{L^{2}}
and
\left({\cdot,\cdot}\right)_{L^{2}({\partial\Omega})}
, respectively.
For
\alpha\in[0,1]
, we write
C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{\Omega})
for the Hölder spaces.
For a real Banach space
X
, a real number
p\in[1,\infty]
and an integer
m\geq 0
, we denote the Bochner spaces by
L^{p}(0,T;X)
and
W^{m,p}(0,T;X)
and they are equipped with the norms
\|{\cdot}\|_{L^{p}(0,T;X)}
and
\|{\cdot}\|_{W^{m,p}(0,T;X)}
. For
p=2
, we will also write
H^{m}(0,T;X)\coloneqq W^{m,2}(0,T;X)
and
\|{\cdot}\|_{H^{m}(0,T;X)}\coloneqq\|{\cdot}\|_{W^{m,2}(0,T;X)}
. Sometimes,
L^{p}(0,T;L^{p})
will be identified with
L^{p}(\Omega_{T})
, where
\Omega_{T}\coloneqq\Omega\times(0,T)
with
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\{2,3\}
, and
T>0
.

2
Derivation and modelling aspects

In this section, we present the derivation of the general viscoelastic model for tumour growth
.
The outline of this section is as follows.
We first present basic balance laws, before we use an energy inequality, a Lagrange multiplier approach and several constitutive assumptions to derive the general viscoelastic model.
We then reformulate the pressure and derive a general energy identity, before we specify the initial and boundary conditions. Then, we give the most relevant examples for the source terms and specify the nutrient and elastic energy densities. After that, we present several variants and limit cases of the model.

2.1
Conservation laws

2.1.1
Balance law of mass

We consider a mixture consisting of healthy and tumour cells. We denote their difference of volume fractions by
\varphi
, with
\{\varphi=1\}
representing the unmixed tumour tissue and
\{\varphi=-1\}
representing the surrounding healthy tissue.
We assume the existence of an unspecified species acting as a nutrient for the tumour whose concentration is denoted by
\sigma
.
Moreover, we assume that
\varphi
and
\sigma
are transported by a volume-averaged velocity
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
and some diffusive fluxes
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}
, respectively.
Based on these assumptions, the balance laws of mass read

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi+\operatorname{div}\left({\varphi\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)+\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\varphi},

(2.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma+\operatorname{div}\left({\sigma\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)+\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{\sigma},

(2.2)

where
\Gamma_{\varphi}
and
\Gamma_{\sigma}
denote the source and sink terms of the phase field variable and the nutrient.
Moreover, mass exchange in terms of the divergence of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
is explicitly given by

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}.

(2.3)

The specific motivation for (
2.1
) and (
2.3
) is based on mass balance laws for the two components of the mixture, i.e. the tumour and healthy cells, and we refer the reader to
[
23
]
for more details.
In the general case, the mass densities of the tumour cells
\bar{\varrho}_{1}
and the healthy cells
\bar{\varrho}_{-1}
can differ, which yields for the mass density
\varrho
of the mixture,

\displaystyle\varrho=\hat{\varrho}(\varphi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{\varrho}_{1}(1+\varphi)+\tfrac{1}{2}\bar{\varrho}_{-1}(1-\varphi).

(2.4)

For simplicity reasons, we consider matching mass densities of the pure components in this work, which results in
\varrho=\bar{\varrho}_{1}=\bar{\varrho}_{-1}
.

2.1.2
Balance law of linear momentum

Motivated by, e.g.,
[
1
,
23
]
, we assume that the mixture is a single viscoelastic fluid that fulfills the balance law of linear momentum of continuum mechanics.
We further neglect any gravity effects or body forces and suppose that contact forces are represented by a viscoelastic stress tensor
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
.
Moreover, we assume that the viscoelastic stress tensor is symmetric, isotropic and can depend on
\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\nabla\varphi
and
\mathbb{B}_{e}
, where
\mathbb{B}_{e}
is the left Cauchy–Green tensor associated with the elastic part of the total mechanical response which will be defined later (see (
2.11
)).
With these assumptions, the balance law of linear momentum is given by

\displaystyle\varrho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=\operatorname{div}\left({\overline{\mathbb{T}}}\right),

(2.5)

where
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
has to specified by constitutive assumptions.

2.1.3
Concept of viscoelasticity

In the literature
[
40
,
41
,
43
]
, a popular approach for viscoelasticity is in terms of the deformation gradient
\mathbb{F}:\Omega\times(0,T)\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
between the initial configuration and the current configuration of a viscoelastic body. Writing
\mathbb{F}
in Eulerian coordinates, we obtain the hyperbolic evolution equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{F}=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{F}.

(2.6)

Hence, it is easily deduced from (
2.6
) that the left Cauchy–Green tensor
\tilde{}\mathbb{B}\coloneqq\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}^{T}
satisfies the evolution equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\tilde{}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\tilde{}\mathbb{B}=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\tilde{}\mathbb{B}+\tilde{}\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.7)

This is the so-called Oldroyd-B equation with infinite Weissenberg number which is a common way to describe viscoelastic materials of Kelvin–Voigt type, where stress relaxation is neglected.
However, stress relaxation is a typical behaviour of living tissues
[
4
]
.
For this reason, we follow the approach of Málek and Průša
[
47
]
in order to derive a viscoelastic approach that accounts for stress relaxation.
We assume a
virtual
framework consisting of three configurations: the initial configuration, the current configuration at time
t>0
and the natural configuration which would be taken by the considered body at time
t>0
after immediate relaxation, see Figure
1
.
Therefore, we assume a
virtual
multiplicative decomposition of the full deformation gradient
\mathbb{F}
by

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{F}_{d},

(2.8)

where
\mathbb{F}_{d}
describes the deformation gradient between the initial and the natural configuration, taking into account only the dissipative processes of the fluid, which, in the biological context, can arise from, e.g., cell reorganizations, birth and death of cells
[
4
]
. Besides,
\mathbb{F}_{e}
measures only the elastic part of the deformation, which is the deformation gradient between the natural and the current configuration.
Then, the sought measure of our main interest is the left Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}_{e}\coloneqq\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}
associated with the elastic part of the deformation.

Figure 1:
Configurations of a viscoelastic cell-mixture within a fixed domain, described by a virtual decomposition of the total deformation. Adapted from
[
47
]
.

Following the ideas of Málek and Průša
[
47
]
, we define
\mathbb{L}\coloneqq\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
and its symmetric part
\mathbb{D}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{L}+\mathbb{L}^{T})
. From (
2.6
) we see that
\mathbb{L}=(\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{F})\mathbb{F}^{-1}
, where the material derivative of
\mathbb{F}
is defined by
\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{F}\coloneqq\partial_{t}\mathbb{F}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{F}
.
This motivates to introduce the tensorial quantity
\mathbb{L}_{d}
by

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{d}\coloneqq(\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{F}_{d})\mathbb{F}_{d}^{-1},

(2.9)

and its symmetric part by
\mathbb{D}_{d}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{L}_{d}+\mathbb{L}_{d}^{T})
.
Together with the formula
\partial_{t}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{F}_{d}^{-1})=-\mathbb{F}_{d}^{-1}(\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{F}_{d})\mathbb{F}_{d}^{-1}
we see that the material derivative of the relative deformation gradient
\mathbb{F}_{e}
is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{F}_{e}=\partial_{t}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}_{d}^{-1})=(\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{F})\mathbb{F}_{d}^{-1}+\mathbb{F}\partial_{t}^{\bullet}(\mathbb{F}_{d}^{-1})=\mathbb{L}\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}_{d}^{-1}-\mathbb{F}\mathbb{F}_{d}^{-1}(\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{F}_{d})\mathbb{F}_{d}^{-1}=\mathbb{L}\mathbb{F}_{e}-\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{L}_{d},

(2.10)

which implies, as
\mathbb{B}_{e}=\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}
, that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{B}_{e}=\mathbb{L}\mathbb{B}_{e}+\mathbb{B}_{e}\mathbb{L}^{T}-2\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{D}_{d}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}.

(2.11)

This is the sought formula for the evolution of the left Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}_{e}
. The right-hand side of (
2.11
) depends on the quantities
\mathbb{F}_{e}
,
\mathbb{D}_{d}
and
\mathbb{L}=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
. Later, the dependency on the tensor
\mathbb{D}_{d}
will be removed by constitutive assumptions.

2.2
Energy inequality and the Lagrange multiplier method

In order to derive the system
from thermodynamical principles, we apply the Lagrange multiplier method by Liu and Müller developed in
[
44
]
. We remark that the mass density
\varrho
is assumed to be constant. In the case of a non-constant mass density given by formula (
2.4
), the derivation of a system of equations can be performed with methods from Abels, Garcke and Grün
[
1
]
.

We postulate a general energy density of the form

\displaystyle e=\hat{e}(\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}_{e})+\frac{1}{2}\varrho\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2},

(2.12)

where
\hat{e}
denotes the free energy density of the system which can depend on
\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}_{e}
, whereas
\frac{1}{2}\varrho\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}
denotes the kinetic energy density.
Let
V(t)\subset\Omega
be an arbitrary volume which is transported with the fluid velocity. We now consider the following energy inequality resulting from the second law of thermodynamics:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\underbrace{\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int_{V(t)}e(\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B}_{e})\,{\mathrm{d}}x}_{\text{ change of energy}}&\leq\underbrace{-\int_{\partial V(t)}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ energy flux across}\\
\text{ the boundary}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\int_{\partial V(t)}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ work due to }\\
\text{ macroscopic stresses}\end{subarray}}\\
&\quad+\underbrace{\int_{V(t)}c_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}+c_{\varphi}\Gamma_{\varphi}+c_{\sigma}(-\Gamma_{\sigma})\,{\mathrm{d}}x}_{\text{supply of energy}},\end{split}

(2.13)

where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}
is the outer unit normal to
\partial V(t)
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{e}
is an energy flux yet to be determined and
c_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
,
c_{\varphi}
and
c_{\sigma}
are unknown multipliers which have to be specified.

Following the arguments in, e.g.,
[
1
,
23
,
31
]
, we introduce Lagrange multipliers
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
,
\lambda_{\varphi}
and
\lambda_{\sigma}
for (
2.3
), (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), respectively.
Using the momentum balance equation (
2.5
) and the Gauss theorem, we can reformulate the boundary integral describing work due to macroscopic stresses by

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial V(t)}(\overline{\mathbb{T}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}=-\int_{V(t)}\operatorname{div}\left({\overline{\mathbb{T}}}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\overline{\mathbb{T}}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=-\int_{V(t)}\varrho\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\overline{\mathbb{T}}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

Therefore, using Reynold’s transport theorem
[
27
]
and the fact that
V(t)
is arbitrary,
we obtain the following local dissipation inequality

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\mathcal{D}&\coloneqq\partial_{t}^{\bullet}e+e\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)+\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{e}}\right)-\overline{\mathbb{T}}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}-\varrho\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}-c_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}-c_{\varphi}\Gamma_{\varphi}+c_{\sigma}\Gamma_{\sigma}\\
&\quad-\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}(\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)-\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}})-\lambda_{\varphi}(\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi+\varphi\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)+\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}}\right)-\Gamma_{\varphi})-\lambda_{\sigma}(\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\sigma+\sigma\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)+\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}}\right)+\Gamma_{\sigma})\\
&\leq 0,\end{split}

(2.14)

which has to hold for arbitrary values of
\varphi
,
\sigma
,
\nabla\varphi
,
\nabla\sigma
,
\varrho
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
,
\mathbb{B}_{e}
,
\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
,
\Gamma_{\varphi}
,
\Gamma_{\sigma}
,
\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi
,
\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\sigma
and
\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
.
By the chain rule, we then have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\bullet}e

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial e}{\partial\varphi}\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi+\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}\cdot\partial_{t}^{\bullet}(\nabla\varphi)+\frac{\partial e}{\partial\sigma}\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\sigma+\frac{\partial e}{\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\cdot\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}:\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{B}_{e}.

Therefore, on noting (
2.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\mathcal{D}&=\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{e}-\lambda_{\varphi}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}-\lambda_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}}\right)+\nabla\lambda_{\varphi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}+\nabla\lambda_{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}+\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}\cdot\partial_{t}^{\bullet}(\nabla\varphi)\\
&\quad+\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\varphi}-\lambda_{\varphi}\Big)+\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\sigma\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\sigma}-\lambda_{\sigma}\Big)+\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}-\varrho\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\Big)\\
&\quad-\mathbb{T}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}:(\mathbb{L}\mathbb{B}_{e}+\mathbb{B}_{e}\mathbb{L}^{T}-2\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{D}_{d}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T})\\
&\quad+(c_{\sigma}-\lambda_{\sigma})\Gamma_{\sigma}+(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}-c_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}})\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}+(\lambda_{\varphi}-c_{\varphi})\Gamma_{\varphi}+(e-\lambda_{\varphi}\varphi-\lambda_{\sigma}\sigma-\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}})\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\\
&\leq 0.\end{split}

(2.15)

Together with
\partial_{x_{j}}(\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi)=\partial_{t}\partial_{x_{j}}\varphi+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla(\partial_{x_{j}}\varphi)+\partial_{x_{j}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla\varphi=\partial_{t}^{\bullet}(\partial_{x_{j}}\varphi)+\partial_{x_{j}}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla\varphi
,
we calculate

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left({\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}}\right)=\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi\operatorname{div}\left({\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}}\right)+\partial_{t}^{\bullet}(\nabla\varphi)\cdot\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}+\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}:\Big(\nabla\varphi\otimes\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}\Big).

Then, using
\frac{\partial e}{\partial\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}=\varrho\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
, we can reformulate (
2.15
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\mathcal{D}&=\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{e}-\lambda_{\varphi}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}-\lambda_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}+\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}}\right)+\nabla\lambda_{\varphi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}+\nabla\lambda_{\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}\\
&\quad+\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\varphi}-\operatorname{div}\left({\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}}\right)-\lambda_{\varphi}\Big)+\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\sigma\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\sigma}-\lambda_{\sigma}\Big)\\
&\quad-\Big(\overline{\mathbb{T}}+\Big(\nabla\varphi\otimes\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}\Big)\Big):\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}:(\mathbb{L}\mathbb{B}_{e}+\mathbb{B}_{e}\mathbb{L}^{T}-2\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{D}_{d}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T})\\
&\quad+(c_{\sigma}-\lambda_{\sigma})\Gamma_{\sigma}+(\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}-c_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}})\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}+(\lambda_{\varphi}-c_{\varphi})\Gamma_{\varphi}+(e-\lambda_{\varphi}\varphi-\lambda_{\sigma}\sigma-\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}})\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\\
&\leq 0.\end{split}

(2.16)

In the following we denote the chemical potential of the order parameter
\varphi
as

\displaystyle\mu\coloneqq\frac{\partial e}{\partial\varphi}-\operatorname{div}\left({\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}}\right).

2.3
Constitutive relations

To fulfill the last inequality for
-\mathcal{D}
, we can argue similarly to
[
1
,
23
,
31
]
and we make the following constitutive assumptions for the fluxes
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{e}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}
, for the constants
c_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
,
c_{\varphi}
,
c_{\sigma}
and for the Lagrange multipliers
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
,
\lambda_{\varphi}
,
\lambda_{\sigma}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{e}&=\lambda_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}+\lambda_{\varphi}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}-\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi},\quad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}=-m(\varphi)\nabla\lambda_{\varphi},\quad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}=-n(\varphi)\nabla\lambda_{\sigma},\\
c_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}&=\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}},\quad c_{\varphi}=\lambda_{\varphi}=\frac{\partial e}{\partial\varphi}-\operatorname{div}\left({\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}}\right)=\mu,\quad c_{\sigma}=\lambda_{\sigma}=\frac{\partial e}{\partial\sigma},\end{split}

(2.17)

where
m(\varphi)
and
n(\varphi)
are non-negative mobilities corresponding to a generalised Fick’s law (see
[
1
]
).

Actually,
m(\varphi)
and
n(\varphi)
could also depend on the chemical potential
\mu
and the nutrient
\sigma
. As pointed out in
[
47
]
, one could also consider cross effects by assuming that the relations between the fluxes
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}
and the gradients of the Lagrange parameters
\nabla\lambda_{\varphi}
,
\nabla\lambda_{\sigma}
take the form

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\alpha}=-\sum_{\beta\in\{\varphi,\sigma\}}\mathrm{M}_{\alpha,\beta}\nabla\lambda_{\beta}
for
\alpha\in\{\varphi,\sigma\}
,
where
(\mathrm{M}_{\alpha,\beta})
is a symmetric positive definite matrix which could depend on, e.g.,
\varphi
,
\mu
and
\sigma
.
This would of course lead to a more complex system of equations, and is hence not explored here.

Under these constitutive assumptions, the inequality for the local dissipation (
2.16
) holds if

\displaystyle\begin{split}&-\Big(\overline{\mathbb{T}}+\Big(\nabla\varphi\otimes\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}\Big)\Big):\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}:(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}_{e}+\mathbb{B}_{e}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}-2\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{D}_{d}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T})\\
&\quad+(e-\lambda_{\varphi}\varphi-\lambda_{\sigma}\sigma-\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}})\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\\
&\leq 0.\end{split}

(2.18)

Using the properties of the trace and the symmetry of
\mathbb{B}_{e}
and
\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}
, we calculate

\displaystyle\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}:(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}_{e})=\trace\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}^{T}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}\Big)=\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}\Big):\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}:\big(\mathbb{B}_{e}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}\big)=\trace\Big(\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\Big)^{T}\mathbb{B}_{e}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}\Big)=\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}\Big):\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}:(\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{D}_{d}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T})=\trace\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{D}_{d}^{T}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\Big)=\trace\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{D}_{d}^{T}\Big)=\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big):\mathbb{D}_{d}.

Therefore, we can reformulate (
2.18
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Big(-\overline{\mathbb{T}}-\Big(\nabla\varphi\otimes\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}\Big)+2\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}\Big):\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}-2\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big):\mathbb{D}_{d}+(e-\lambda_{\varphi}\varphi-\lambda_{\sigma}\sigma-\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}})\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\leq 0.\end{split}

(2.19)

Introducing the unknown pressure
p
, we can rewrite the stress tensor
\mathbb{T}
as follows:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{T}}=\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I},\quad\text{ i.e. }\quad\mathbb{S}=\overline{\mathbb{T}}+p\mathbb{I}.

(2.20)

Similar arguments as in
[
23
]
imply that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}=a(\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}_{e})\nabla\varphi,

(2.21)

where
a(\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}_{e})
is a real valued function. Since
\mathbb{S}
is symmetric, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=\mathbb{S}:\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T})+\mathbb{S}:\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}-(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T})=\mathbb{S}:\mathbb{D},

and similarly, as
\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}
and
\mathbb{B}_{e}
are symmetric, we also obtain

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}\Big):\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}\Big):\mathbb{D}.

Using the identity
\mathbb{I}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=\mathbb{I}:\mathbb{D}=\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{T}}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=(\mathbb{S}-p\mathbb{I}):\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=\mathbb{S}:\mathbb{D}-p\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right).

This yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Big(-\mathbb{S}-a(\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}_{e})(\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi)+2\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}\Big):\mathbb{D}\\
&\quad-2\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big):\mathbb{D}_{d}+(e-\lambda_{\varphi}\varphi-\lambda_{\sigma}\sigma+p-\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}})\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\leq 0.\end{split}

(2.22)

The quantities
\mathbb{D}
and
\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)
appear in the first and in the last term on the left-hand side of (
2.22
) as it holds
\trace\mathbb{D}=\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)
. Therefore, these two quantities are not independent. As pointed out in
[
47
]
, it would actually be necessary to split
\mathbb{D}
to mutually independent quantities consisting of the traceless part of
\mathbb{D}
and the part containing
\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)
, which requires one to split the quantities in the first brackets in (
2.22
) in a similar manner.
However, following the strategy of
[
23
,
31
]
, we choose the constitutive assumption

\displaystyle\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\coloneqq e-\lambda_{\varphi}\varphi-\lambda_{\sigma}\sigma+p,

(2.23)

for the Lagrange parameter
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
in order to control the mass exchange term even though
\mathbb{D}
and
\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)
are not independent quantities. The reason for this is that we can reformulate the unknown pressure
p
and therefore adapt the constitutive assumption for the Lagrange parameter
\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
afterwards.
Hence, it remains to fulfill the inequality

\displaystyle\begin{split}&-\Big(\mathbb{S}+a(\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}_{e})(\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi)-2\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}\Big):\mathbb{D}-2\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big):\mathbb{D}_{d}\leq 0.\end{split}

(2.24)

At this point we make the constitutive assumptions for
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{D}_{d}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}

\displaystyle=2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{D}+\lambda(\varphi)\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\mathbb{I}+2\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}-a(\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}_{e})(\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi),

(2.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{-1}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big),

(2.26)

with non-negative viscosities
\eta(\cdot)
,
\lambda(\cdot)
and a non-negative viscoelastic relaxation function
\tau(\cdot)
, which could also depend on
\mu
and
\sigma
.

Noting that
\lambda(\varphi)\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\mathbb{I}:\mathbb{D}=\lambda(\varphi)\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)^{2}
and

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big):\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{-1}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big)=\trace\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\Big(\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\Big)^{T}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{-T}\Big)=\left\lvert{\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}}\right\rvert^{2},

we obtain that the local dissipation inequality is fulfilled, i.e.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{D}&=2\eta(\varphi)\left\lvert{\mathbb{D}}\right\rvert^{2}+\lambda(\varphi)(\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right))^{2}+m(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla\mu}\right\rvert^{2}+n(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla\frac{\partial e}{\partial\sigma}}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{2}{\tau(\varphi)}\left\lvert{\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}}\right\rvert^{2}\geq 0.\end{split}

(2.27)

So, dissipation can be divided into the following processes: viscosity effects on the velocity (i.e.
2\eta(\varphi)\left\lvert{\mathbb{D}}\right\rvert^{2}
),
changes in volume (i.e.
\lambda(\varphi)(\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right))^{2}
),
transport along
\nabla\mu
and
\nabla\frac{\partial e}{\partial\sigma}
, and dissipation caused by viscoelastic relaxation (i.e.
\frac{2}{\tau(\varphi)}\left\lvert{\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}}\right\rvert^{2}
).

We remark that multiplying (
2.26
) with
\mathbb{F}_{e}
from the left and with
\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}
from the right yields a formula for
\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{D}_{d}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{D}_{d}\mathbb{F}^{T}_{e}=\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{F}^{T}_{e}=\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}.

(2.28)

Combining (
2.28
) and (
2.11
) leads to the following constitutive equation for the left Cauchy–Green tensor:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{B}_{e}+\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}_{e}+\mathbb{B}_{e}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.29)

This can be seen as a generalized viscoelastic model of Oldroyd-B type
[
51
]
.

Instead of the constitutive assumption (
2.26
), it is also possible to assume

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{d}=\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big),

(2.30a)

which, after multiplication with
\mathbb{F}_{e}
from the left and with
\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}
from the right, using
\mathbb{B}_{e}=\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}
and noting (
2.11
), leads to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{B}_{e}+\frac{2}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{B}_{e}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}_{e}+\mathbb{B}_{e}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.31)

This can be seen as a generalized version of the viscoelastic model of Giesekus
[
33
]
.
In this case, the local dissipation is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{D}&=2\eta(\varphi)\left\lvert{\mathbb{D}}\right\rvert^{2}+\lambda(\varphi)(\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right))^{2}+m(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla\mu}\right\rvert^{2}+n(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla\frac{\partial e}{\partial\sigma}}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{2}{\tau(\varphi)}\left\lvert{\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}}\right\rvert^{2}\geq 0,\end{split}

(2.32)

instead of (
2.27
).
However, the focus of this work lies on the constitutive relation (
2.26
) leading to the viscoelastic model of Oldroyd-B type.

We now summarize all the constitutive assumptions from this section:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{e}=\lambda_{\sigma}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}+\lambda_{\varphi}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}-\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi},\quad\quad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\varphi}=-m(\varphi)\nabla\lambda_{\varphi},\quad\quad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{J}}_{\sigma}=-n(\varphi)\nabla\lambda_{\sigma},\\
&c_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}=\lambda_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}=e-\lambda_{\varphi}\varphi-\lambda_{\sigma}\sigma+p,\quad\quad c_{\varphi}=\lambda_{\varphi}=\frac{\partial e}{\partial\varphi}-\operatorname{div}\left({\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}}\right)=\mu,\quad\quad c_{\sigma}=\lambda_{\sigma}=\frac{\partial e}{\partial\sigma},\\
&\Big(\mathbb{S}+a(\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}_{e})(\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi)-2\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{B}_{e}\Big)=2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{D}+\lambda(\varphi)\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\mathbb{I},\\
&\mathbb{D}_{d}=\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{-1}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big),\quad\text{or}\quad\mathbb{D}_{d}=\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big).\end{split}

(2.33)

2.4
Further aspects of modelling

2.4.1
The model equations

From now on we suppress the index of
\mathbb{B}_{e}
, i.e. we write
\mathbb{B}
instead of
\mathbb{B}_{e}
, and we also write
\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})
instead of
\mathbb{D}
to point out the dependency on
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
.
In the following, we assume a general energy density of the form

\displaystyle e(\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})=f(\varphi,\nabla\varphi)+N(\varphi,\sigma)+W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})+\frac{1}{2}\varrho\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}.

(2.34)

The first term
f(\varphi,\nabla\varphi)
in (
2.34
) accounts for interfacial energy of the diffuse interface
[
17
]

which we assume to be of Ginzburg–Landau type:

\displaystyle f(\varphi,\nabla\varphi)=A\psi(\varphi)+\frac{B}{2}\left\lvert{\nabla\varphi}\right\rvert^{2},

(2.35)

where
\psi(\cdot)
is a non-negative potential with equal minima at
\varphi=\pm 1
, and
A,B>0
are constants. Usually we set
A=\frac{\beta}{\epsilon}
and
B=\beta\epsilon
, where the constants
\beta,\epsilon>0
are related to the surface tension and the interfacial thickness, respectively.

The second term
N(\varphi,\sigma)
in (
2.34
) describes the energy contribution due to the presence of the nutrient and the interaction between the tumour tissues and the nutrients, also see
[
31
]
. The third term
W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})
in (
2.34
) represents the elastic part of the energy which we additionally assume to depend on the type of material and hence on
\varphi
.
For the moment, both the nutrient and the elastic energy density are kept in a general form, but later, possible choices are given.
The last term in (
2.34
) is the kinetic part of the energy.

With these choices we calculate

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\partial e}{\partial\varphi}=A\psi^{\prime}(\varphi)+N_{,\varphi}+W_{,\varphi},\quad\frac{\partial e}{\partial\nabla\varphi}=B\nabla\varphi,\quad\frac{\partial e}{\partial\sigma}=N_{,\sigma},\quad\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}}=W_{,\mathbb{B}},\quad a(\varphi,\nabla\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B})=B,\end{split}

(2.36)

where
N_{,\varphi}
, and
N_{,\sigma}
denote the partial derivatives of
N(\varphi,\sigma)
with respect to
\varphi
and
\sigma
. For better readability, note that we sometimes suppress the arguments of
N_{,\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma),N_{,\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)
and we write
N_{,\varphi},N_{,\sigma}
instead. Similarly, we adopt the notation for
W
.
Next, we specify the constitutive relation for the full stress tensor
\overline{\mathbb{T}}=-p\mathbb{I}+\mathbb{S}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{\mathbb{T}}&=-p\mathbb{I}+2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})+\lambda(\varphi)\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\mathbb{I}+2W_{,\mathbb{B}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\mathbb{B}-B\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi.\end{split}

(2.37)

Collecting all equations from above, the general viscoelastic model of Oldroyd-B type reads:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi+\operatorname{div}\left({\varphi\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({m(\varphi)\nabla\mu}\right)+\Gamma_{\varphi},

(2.38a)

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=A\psi^{\prime}(\varphi)-B\Delta\varphi+N_{,\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma)+W_{,\varphi}(\varphi,\mathbb{B}),

(2.38b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma+\operatorname{div}\left({\sigma\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({n(\varphi)\nabla N_{,\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)}\right)-\Gamma_{\sigma},

(2.38c)

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}},

(2.38d)

\displaystyle\varrho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p,\mathbb{B})}\right)-\operatorname{div}\left({B\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi}\right),

(2.38e)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.38f)

For future reference, the full viscoelastic stress tensor is denoted by

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p,\mathbb{B})\coloneqq\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{visc}}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p)+\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B}),

(2.39)

where the viscous and the elastic parts of the stress tensor are defined as

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{visc}}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\eta(\varphi)\big(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}\big)+\lambda(\varphi)\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\mathbb{I}-p\mathbb{I},

(2.40)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2W_{,\mathbb{B}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\mathbb{B}.

(2.41)

Note that
\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p,\mathbb{B})
corresponds to
\overline{\mathbb{T}}
without the last term in (
2.37
).

As remarked in the derivation, a viscoelastic description of Giesekus type is also possible, which then leads to the system of equations (
2.38a
)–(
2.38e
) together with the constitutive equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{B}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.42)

However, the focus in this work lies on the viscoelastic description of Oldroyd-B type.

2.4.2
Reformulations of the pressure

We consider the following two reformulations of the pressure leading to a variant of (
2.38e
). For more examples, see
[
23
,
31
]
.

•

Using the fact that
\nabla\big(\frac{B}{2}\left\lvert{\nabla\varphi}\right\rvert^{2}\big)=\operatorname{div}\left({B\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi}\right)-B\Delta\varphi\nabla\varphi
and defining
q\coloneqq p+f(\varphi,\nabla\varphi)+N(\varphi,\sigma)
yields

\displaystyle\nabla q=\nabla p+(\mu-W_{,\varphi})\nabla\varphi+N_{,\sigma}\nabla\sigma+\operatorname{div}\left({B\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi}\right).

We can hence write (
2.38e
) as

\displaystyle\varrho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=\operatorname{div}\left({\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},q,\mathbb{B})}\right)+(\mu-W_{,\varphi})\nabla\varphi+N_{,\sigma}\nabla\sigma.

(2.43)

Let us mention that the system (
2.38a
)–(
2.38f
) with (
2.38e
) replaced by (
2.43
) matches with the general viscoelastic model
. Moreover, replacing (
2.38e
) by (
2.43
) makes it possible that the convection terms in (
2.38a
) and (
2.38c
) cancel out within specific testing procedures.

•

The following reformulation is of great importance when dealing with quasi-static nutrient equations, see
[
23
]
.
Setting
q\coloneqq p+f(\varphi,\nabla\varphi)
yields

\displaystyle\nabla q=\nabla p+(\mu-N_{,\varphi}-W_{,\varphi})\nabla\varphi+\operatorname{div}\left({B\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi}\right),

so that (
2.38e
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varrho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=\operatorname{div}\left({\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},q,\mathbb{B})}\right)+(\mu-N_{,\varphi}-W_{,\varphi})\nabla\varphi.\end{split}

(2.44)

2.4.3
A general energy identity

In the following, we derive a general energy identity for the viscoelastic model of Oldroyd-B type (
2.38a
)–(
2.38f
),
where we write (
2.43
) instead of (
2.38e
) and we write
p
instead of
q
, such that the convection terms in (
2.38a
) and (
2.38c
) cancel out within the following testing procedure.

Let us temporarily assume that there exists a sufficiently smooth solution of the above system. We multiply (
2.38a
) with
\mu
, (
2.38b
) with
-\partial_{t}\varphi
and (
2.38c
) with
N_{,\sigma}
, integrate over
\Omega
and use Green’s formula. We then obtain:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\varphi\mu+\mu\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varphi\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\mu+m(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla\mu}\right\rvert^{2}-\Gamma_{\varphi}\mu\,{\mathrm{d}}x-\int_{\partial\Omega}m(\varphi)\mu\nabla\mu\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1},

(2.45)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\varphi(\mu-N_{,\varphi}-W_{,\varphi})\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int_{\Omega}A\psi(\varphi)+\frac{B}{2}\left\lvert{\nabla\varphi}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x-\int_{\partial\Omega}B\partial_{t}\varphi\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1},

(2.46)

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\sigma N_{,\sigma}+N_{,\sigma}\nabla\sigma\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+N_{,\sigma}\sigma\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}+n(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla N_{,\sigma}}\right\rvert^{2}+\Gamma_{\sigma}N_{,\sigma}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\quad-\int_{\partial\Omega}n(\varphi)N_{,\sigma}\nabla N_{,\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}.\end{split}

(2.47)

Next, we multiply (
2.43
) with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
and integrate over
\Omega
and use Green’s formula so that we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\int_{\Omega}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\left(\frac{1}{2}\varrho\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}\right)+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+2\eta(\varphi)\left\lvert{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\right\rvert^{2}+\lambda(\varphi)(\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right))^{2}-p\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega}(-(\mu-W_{,\varphi})\nabla\varphi-N_{,\sigma}\nabla\sigma)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+2(W_{,\mathbb{B}}\mathbb{B}):\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x-\int_{\partial\Omega}\big(\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p,\mathbb{B})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}\big)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}.\end{split}

(2.48)

Here we used that
\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}):\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}):\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})
and
\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\mathbb{I}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=(\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right))^{2}
.
After that, we multiply (
2.38f
) with
W_{,\mathbb{B}}
and we integrate over
\Omega
and apply Green’s formula. This yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Big(\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}-\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}+\frac{2}{\tau(\varphi)}W_{,\mathbb{B}}\mathbb{B}\Big):W_{,\mathbb{B}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

(2.49)

For the reader’s convenience, we now note some useful identities concerning the velocity and the Cauchy–Green tensor:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\varrho\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\varrho\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\big(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}\big):W_{,\mathbb{B}}

\displaystyle=2(W_{,\mathbb{B}}\mathbb{B}):\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\bullet}W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\varphi W_{,\varphi}+\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{B}:W_{,\mathbb{B}},

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

\displaystyle(W_{,\mathbb{B}}\mathbb{B}):W_{,\mathbb{B}}

\displaystyle\geq 0,\quad\text{ if }\mathbb{B}\text{ is positive definite.}

Collecting all equations (
2.45
)–(
2.49
), we obtain the general energy identity for the viscoelastic model for tumour growth:

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\Big(\int_{\Omega}A\psi(\varphi)+\frac{B}{2}\left\lvert{\nabla\varphi}\right\rvert^{2}+N(\varphi,\sigma)+W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})+\frac{1}{2}\varrho\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\Big)\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega}m(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla\mu}\right\rvert^{2}+n(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla N_{,\sigma}}\right\rvert^{2}+2\eta(\varphi)\left\lvert{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\right\rvert^{2}+\lambda(\varphi)(\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right))^{2}+\frac{2}{\tau(\varphi)}(W_{,\mathbb{B}}\mathbb{B}):W_{,\mathbb{B}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega}-\Gamma_{\varphi}\mu+\Gamma_{\sigma}N_{,\sigma}+\Big(\mu\varphi+N_{,\sigma}\sigma-p-W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})-\frac{1}{2}\varrho\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}\Big)\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\quad-\int_{\partial\Omega}m(\varphi)\mu\nabla\mu\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}+n(\varphi)N_{,\sigma}\nabla N_{,\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}+B\partial_{t}\varphi\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\\
&\quad+\int_{\partial\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\Big(W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})+\frac{1}{2}\varrho\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}\Big)-\big(\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p,\mathbb{B})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}\big)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}.\end{split}

(2.50)

Note that in order to study existence theory, there are several difficulties that arise from this general identity and heavily depend on the choices for the potential
\psi(\varphi)
, the energy densities
N(\varphi,\sigma),W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})
, the source terms
\Gamma_{\varphi},\Gamma_{\sigma},\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
, the functions
m(\varphi),n(\varphi),\eta(\varphi),\lambda(\varphi),\tau(\varphi)
and the initial and boundary conditions.

2.4.4
Initial and boundary conditions

For
\varphi
,
\sigma
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
and
\mathbb{B}
, we impose the initial conditions

\displaystyle\varphi(\cdot,0)=\varphi_{0},\quad\sigma(\cdot,0)=\sigma_{0},\quad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}(\cdot,0)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0},\quad\mathbb{B}(\cdot,0)=\mathbb{B}_{0}\quad\text{ a.e.\penalty\ in }\Omega.

(2.51)

We prescribe homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions on
\partial\Omega
for the phase field variable and the chemical potential, i.e.

\displaystyle\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}=\nabla\mu\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}=0\quad\text{ a.e.\penalty\ on }\partial\Omega\times(0,T).

(2.52)

For the nutrient we prescribe Robin-type boundary conditions

\displaystyle n(\varphi)\nabla N_{,\sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}=K(\sigma_{\infty}-\sigma)\quad\text{ a.e.\penalty\ on }\partial\Omega\times(0,T),

(2.53)

where the constant
K\geq 0
is referred to as the boundary permeability and
\sigma_{\infty}
denotes a given nutrient supply at the boundary.

The last boundary condition is depending on the choice of
\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
. In this work, we consider
\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}=0
. Hence, we prescribe no-slip (homogeneous Dirichlet) boundary conditions for the velocity, i.e.

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}=\boldsymbol{0}\quad\text{ a.e.\penalty\ on }\partial\Omega\times(0,T).

(2.54)

In the case
\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\not=0
, we prescribe the following boundary condition for
\mathbb{T}
,

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p,\mathbb{B})-W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\mathbb{I}-\frac{1}{2}\varrho\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}\mathbb{I}\Big)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}=\boldsymbol{0}\quad\text{ a.e.\penalty\ on }\partial\Omega\times(0,T),

(2.55)

so that the last line in the general energy identity (
2.50
) vanishes.

In the case of no-slip boundary conditions for the velocity, we recall that no further boundary conditions for the Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}
are needed, as the evolution equation (
2.38f
) is a hyperbolic partial differential equation of first order and has no incoming characteristics at the boundary.

2.4.5
Specific choices for the source terms

Now, we explain possible specifications for the source terms
\Gamma_{\varphi},\Gamma_{\sigma},\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
.

•

Usually the source terms
\Gamma_{\varphi}
and
\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
are closely related. In particular,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\bar{\varrho}_{1}}\Gamma_{1}-\frac{1}{\bar{\varrho}_{-1}}\Gamma_{-1},\qquad\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\bar{\varrho}_{1}}\Gamma_{1}+\frac{1}{\bar{\varrho}_{-1}}\Gamma_{-1},

(2.56)

where
\bar{\varrho}_{1},\bar{\varrho}_{-1}
are the mass densities of the tumour cells and healthy cells, respectively, and
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{-1}
are source or sink terms in the mass balance laws for the single components of the mixture, see
[
23
,
31
]
. By the assumption of matching mass densities, we have
\varrho=\bar{\varrho}_{1}=\bar{\varrho}_{-1}
and hence

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi}=\frac{1}{\varrho}(\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{-1}),\qquad\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}=\frac{1}{\varrho}(\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{-1}).

(2.57)

A common choice of
\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
is obtained by assuming no gain or loss of mass locally, i.e.
\Gamma_{1}\coloneqq-\Gamma_{-1}
, which implicates

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi}=\frac{2}{\varrho}\Gamma_{1},\qquad\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}=0.

(2.58)

On the other hand, setting
\Gamma_{-1}\coloneqq 0
yields

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi}=\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}=\frac{1}{\varrho}\Gamma_{1}.

(2.59)

•

Motivated by linear kinetics, Garcke and co-authors
[
31
]
suggested

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma)\coloneqq(\mathcal{P}\sigma-\mathcal{A})h(\varphi),\qquad\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)\coloneqq\mathcal{C}\sigma h(\varphi)+\mathcal{B}(\sigma_{B}-\sigma),

(2.60)

where
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}
denote the proliferation rate, apoptosis rate and consumption rate.
Moreover,
h(\cdot)
is an interpolation function with
h(-1)=0
and
h(1)=1
which ensures that proliferation, apoptosis and nutrient consumption only take place in the tumour phase. The simplest example ist
h(\varphi)=\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\varphi)
.
Besides,
\mathcal{B}(\sigma_{B}-\sigma)
models the nutrient supply from an existing vasculature.

•

To account for the influence of mechanical stresses on tissue growth, the authors of
[
30
]
proposed to scale the proliferation term
\mathcal{P}\sigma h(\varphi)
in (
2.60
) with
\tilde{f}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}})\coloneqq(1+\left\lvert{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}}\right\rvert^{2})^{-1/2}
,
which decreases when elastic stresses increase.
This motivates to introduce the choice

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B})\coloneqq\big(\mathcal{P}\sigma f(\varphi,\mathbb{B})-\mathcal{A}\big)h(\varphi),\quad\text{where}\quad f(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\coloneqq\left(1+\left\lvert{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})}\right\rvert^{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(2.61)

where
h(\cdot),\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A}
are as in (
2.60
).

•

Based on linear phenomenological laws for chemical reactions, the authors of
[
37
]
proposed to take

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\mu,\sigma)=\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\mu,\sigma)\coloneqq P(\varphi)\big(N_{,\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)-\mu\big),

(2.62)

with a non-negative proliferation function
P(\cdot)
, e.g.,
P(\varphi)=\max\left\{0,\delta P_{0}(1+\varphi)\right\}
, where
\delta,P_{0}
are positive constants.
For a different example of
P(\cdot)
, we refer to, e.g.,
[
23
]
.

2.4.6
Specific choices for the nutrient and elastic energy density

In the following, we specify the nutrient energy density and give several examples for the elastic energy density.

•

In the literature, the nutrient energy density usually takes the form

\displaystyle N(\varphi,\sigma)\coloneqq\frac{\chi_{\sigma}}{2}\left\lvert{\sigma}\right\rvert^{2}+\chi_{\varphi}\sigma(1-\varphi),

(2.63)

with

\displaystyle N_{,\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma)=-\chi_{\varphi}\sigma,\qquad N_{,\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)=\chi_{\sigma}\sigma-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi.

The first term
\frac{\chi_{\sigma}}{2}\left\lvert{\sigma}\right\rvert^{2}
increases the energy in the presence of nutrients, where
\chi_{\sigma}>0
denotes the diffusivity of the nutrient.
The second term
\chi_{\varphi}\sigma(1-\varphi)
can be regarded as chemotaxis energy which accounts for interactions between the tumour and the nutrient. Here, the constant
\chi_{\varphi}\geq 0
can be seen as a sensitivity parameter for chemotaxis and active uptake mechanisms which favours unstable tumour growth
[
31
]
. Let us point out that the nutrient energy density can have a negative sign in general if
\chi_{\varphi}\not=0
, which is one difficulty in the derivation of suitable
a priori
estimates from the general energy identity (
2.50
).

•

Physically motivated by the theory of constitutive relations for isotropic compressible elastic materials, where large elastic stresses are penalized, the elastic energy density is supposed to satisfy

\displaystyle W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\to+\infty,\quad\text{if}\quad\begin{cases}\left\lvert{\mathbb{B}}\right\rvert\to+\infty,\\
\det(\mathbb{B})\to 0.\end{cases}

(2.64)

Hence, an infinite amount of energy is required such that the material can be expanded to infinite volume or compressed to a single point
[
38
]
.
Note that
\mathbb{B}\coloneqq\mathbb{B}_{e}=\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}
is always symmetric and positive semi-definite by definition while the elastic part of the deformation gradient
\mathbb{F}_{e}
is not symmetric in general.

•

An example for the elastic energy density we have in mind is

\displaystyle W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\kappa(\varphi)\trace(\mathbb{B})-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{0}(\varphi)\ln(\det\mathbb{B}),

(2.65)

with

\displaystyle W_{,\varphi}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})=\frac{1}{2}\kappa^{\prime}(\varphi)\trace(\mathbb{B})-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{0}^{\prime}(\varphi)\ln(\det\mathbb{B}),\qquad W_{,\mathbb{B}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})=\frac{1}{2}\kappa(\varphi)\mathbb{I}-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{0}(\varphi)\mathbb{B}^{-1},

where
\kappa(\varphi),\kappa_{0}(\varphi)>0
denote elasticity parameter functions
depending on the material and
\mathbb{I}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
denotes the identity matrix.
Hence, the elastic stress tensor is
\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})=\kappa(\varphi)\mathbb{B}-\kappa_{0}(\varphi)\mathbb{I}
and (
2.38f
) is specified by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\left(\kappa(\varphi)\mathbb{B}-\kappa_{0}(\varphi)\mathbb{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.66)

In the case
\kappa(\varphi)=\kappa_{0}(\varphi)
and for fixed
\varphi
, (
2.66
) is exactly the classical viscoelastic Oldroyd-B equation
[
6
,
47
]
for the left Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}
.

•

For a given
b\gg 1
, the elastic energy density for the viscoelastic FENE-P model
[
7
]
reads

\displaystyle W(\mathbb{B})\coloneqq-\frac{b}{2}\ln\left(1-\frac{\trace\mathbb{B}}{b}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\trace(\ln\mathbb{B}),\qquad\text{with}\qquad W_{,\mathbb{B}}(\mathbb{B})=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{\trace\mathbb{B}}{b}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{I}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{-1}.

(2.67)

Note that
W(\mathbb{B})\to+\infty
if
\trace(\mathbb{B})\to b
or if
\mathbb{B}
becomes singular.
Here, the corresponding elastic stress tensor is
\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\mathbb{B})=\left(1-\frac{\trace\mathbb{B}}{b}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}
and the constitutive law (
2.38f
) reads

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\left(\left(1-\frac{\trace\mathbb{B}}{b}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.68)

Moreover, the Oldroyd-B equation (
2.66
) with
\kappa(\varphi)=\kappa_{0}(\varphi)=1
can be recovered by formally sending
b\to+\infty
.

•

The authors of
[
16
]
study a generalized viscoelastic Peterlin model for phase separation which is based on the elastic energy density

\displaystyle W(\mathbb{B})\coloneqq\frac{1}{4}\trace(\mathbb{B})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\trace(\ln\mathbb{B})\qquad\text{with}\qquad W_{,\mathbb{B}}(\mathbb{B})=\frac{1}{2}\trace(\mathbb{B})\mathbb{I}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{-1},

(2.69)

and the elastic stress tensor
\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\mathbb{B})=\trace(\mathbb{B})\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}
. Moreover, the tensor
\mathbb{B}
satisfies a generalized evolution equation of the form

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+f(\varphi)g(\trace\mathbb{B})\left(\trace(\mathbb{B})\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T},

(2.70)

where
(f(\varphi)g(\trace\mathbb{B}))^{-1}
denotes a generalized relaxation time depending on the phase field variable
\varphi
and the trace of
\mathbb{B}
.
Besides, a more generalized approach has been studied in
[
46
]
which includes several viscoelastic models.

The elastic energy densities (
2.65
), (
2.67
), (
2.69
) in the one dimensional case are visualized in Figure
2
.

1
2
3
1
2
Oldroyd-B
FENE-P
Peterlin
x

Figure 2:
The elastic energy densities for the Oldroyd-B model (
2.65
) with
\kappa=\kappa_{0}=1
(black), the FENE-P model (
2.67
) with
b=3.5
(blue) and the generalized Peterlin model (
2.69
) (red) in the one dimensional case.

2.4.7
Including growth in the equation for
\mathbb{B}

Mechanical stresses increase when tumour cells proliferate
[
4
]
. Therefore, instead of (
2.38f
), we may consider

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}-\gamma(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\mathbb{B})\mathbb{B},

(2.71)

where the scalar function
\gamma(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\mathbb{B})
acts as a source or sink term for the left Cauchy–Green tensor and can depend on
\varphi
,
\mu
,
\sigma
and
\mathbb{B}
.

This source term can be derived from the multiplicative decomposition

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{F}_{d}\mathbb{F}_{g},

(2.72)

where
\mathbb{F}_{g}
describes deformation by growth
[
4
]
. Assuming spherical growth, i.e.
\mathbb{F}_{g}=g\mathbb{I}
, then, analogously to (
2.10
), we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{F}_{e}=\mathbb{L}\mathbb{F}_{e}-\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{L}_{d}-\mathbb{F}_{e}(\partial_{t}^{\bullet}g)\frac{1}{g},

(2.73)

and, as
\mathbb{B}_{e}=\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\bullet}\mathbb{B}_{e}=\mathbb{L}\mathbb{B}_{e}+\mathbb{B}_{e}\mathbb{L}^{T}-2\mathbb{F}_{e}\mathbb{D}_{d}\mathbb{F}_{e}^{T}-2\mathbb{B}_{e}(\partial_{t}^{\bullet}g)\frac{1}{g},

(2.74)

which coincides with
[
4
, eq. (3.29)]
. Then, (
2.71
) is recovered with

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{d}=\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{-1}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big),\qquad\partial_{t}^{\bullet}g=\frac{1}{2}g\gamma(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\mathbb{B}_{e}).

(2.75)

A different way to obtain (
2.71
) can be motivated by the constitutive choice

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\Big(\mathbb{F}_{e}^{-1}\frac{\partial e}{\partial\mathbb{B}_{e}}\mathbb{F}_{e}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\mathbb{B}_{e})\mathbb{I},

(2.76)

instead of (
2.26
), and by inserting this into (
2.11
).
On the right-hand side of (
2.76
), the first term accounts for stress relaxation while the second term is responsible for growth induced stresses.

In
[
4
]
, it has been suggested that
\gamma
is proportional to the source function
\Gamma_{\varphi}
.
Therefore, we propose the choice

\displaystyle\gamma(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\mathbb{B})=c\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\mathbb{B}),

(2.77)

where
\Gamma_{\varphi}
can be given by (
2.60
), (
2.61
) or (
2.62
), and
c\in\mathbb{R}
.

2.5
Variants of the model

Now, we present several variants of the model (
2.38a
)–(
2.38f
) and exemplify the motivation of these variants with strategies of related works in the literature.

2.5.1
Limit of a small Reynolds number

In biological processes, the Reynolds number is often very small. Then, a non-dimensionalization argument motivates to neglect the terms
\varrho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
in the momentum equation. Hence, we introduce the viscoelastic model with quasi-static momentum equation, which is the system (
2.38a
)–(
2.38f
) with (
2.38e
) replaced by

\displaystyle-\operatorname{div}\left({2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})+\lambda(\varphi)\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)\mathbb{I}}\right)+\nabla p=\operatorname{div}\left({\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})}\right)+(\mu-W_{,\varphi})\nabla\varphi+N_{,\sigma}\nabla\sigma.

(2.78)

In absence of the elastic effects, this model corresponds to a special case of the Cahn–Hilliard–Brinkman model for tumour growth which has been extensively studied in, e.g.,
[
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
]
.

2.5.2
Limit of a short nutrient diffusion timescale

From the modelling point of view, sometimes a quasi-static nutrient equation instead of (
2.38c
) seems realistic since the timescale of nutrient diffusion can be quite small compared to the tumour doubling timescale.
Such approaches have been introduced for related models in the literature, e.g., for the Cahn–Hilliard–Brinkman model
[
23
]
or the Cahn–Hilliard–Darcy model
[
31
]
.

Hence, we introduce the viscoelastic model with quasi-static nutrients which corresponds to (
2.38a
)–(
2.38f
) with (
2.38c
) replaced by

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({n(\varphi)\nabla N_{,\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)}\right)-\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma).

(2.79)

2.5.3
Interpolation between different rheologies

The main concept of viscoelastic models is that both viscous and elastic effects are taken into account. In the context of tumour growth, Bresch and co-authors
[
15
]
proposed a viscoelastic multiphase tumour model of Oldroyd-B type in presence of healthy cells, tumour cells and extracellular liquids, where the material parameters depend on the phases. For example, healthy cells are considered to be more elastic, extracellular liquids are supposed to be fully viscous and tumour cells are assumed to combine both elastic and viscous properties.

We now illustrate the idea of different material laws on the basis of the model (
2.38a
)–(
2.38f
) with the help of suitable choices of the viscosities
\eta(\varphi),\lambda(\varphi)
and the relaxation time
\tau(\varphi)
. We can account for a Newtonian fluid without elastic stresses by sending the relaxation time
\tau(\varphi)
to zero, which leads to (
2.38a
)–(
2.38f
) with (
2.38e
)–(
2.38f
) replaced by

\displaystyle\varrho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{visc}}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p)}\right)-\operatorname{div}\left({B\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi}\right),

(2.80)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})
