\displaystyle=0.

(2.81)

Besides, we can allow a viscoelastic description of Maxwell type by neglecting the viscosities
\eta(\varphi),\lambda(\varphi)
. Hence, the model corresponds to (
2.38a
)–(
2.38f
) with (
2.38e
)–(
2.38f
) replaced by

\displaystyle\varrho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\nabla p

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})}\right)-\operatorname{div}\left({B\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi}\right),

(2.82)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.83)

Moreover, by sending the relaxation time
\tau(\varphi)
to infinity, we obtain the viscoelastic material law of Kelvin–Voigt type and hence recover the Oldroyd-B equation with infinite Weissenberg number (
2.7
) from (
2.38f
) in the limit
\tau(\varphi)\to\infty
, i.e. the model corresponds to (
2.38a
)–(
2.38d
) with

\displaystyle\varrho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({\mathbb{T}(\varphi,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},p,\mathbb{B})}\right)-\operatorname{div}\left({B\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi}\right),

(2.84)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.85)

Further, the material law for an elastic solid can be obtained by neglecting the viscosities and assuming an infinite relaxation time. Hence, the model reads (
2.38a
)–(
2.38d
) combined with

\displaystyle\varrho\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\varrho(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+\nabla p

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})}\right)-\operatorname{div}\left({B\nabla\varphi\otimes\nabla\varphi}\right),

(2.86)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.87)

Of course, we can handle different material laws for the respective phases
\varphi=1
and
\varphi=-1
at once by specifying the viscosities and the relaxation time for the respective phases.
An overview can be found in Table
1
, which has been adapted from
[
50
]
.

Different material laws can be obtained by a different choice of the viscosities and the relaxation time, where ‘

’ marks parameters that are given by the physical problem itself; adapted from
[

.

2.5.4
Evolution of the elastic stress tensor

In the literature, viscoelastic models related to the works of Oldroyd
[
51
]
or Giesekus
[
33
]
are sometimes stated in terms of the elastic stress tensor
\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})=2W_{,\mathbb{B}}(\varphi,\mathbb{B})\mathbb{B}
instead of the left Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}
.
Therefore, we shortly explain how the evolution of the elastic stress tensor is resulting from the evolution equation of the left Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}
for the case
W(\mathbb{B})=\frac{1}{2}\kappa\trace(\mathbb{B}-\ln\mathbb{B})
, where, for simplicity,
\kappa
is constant.
Then, in the Oldroyd-B model, the evolution equation (
2.38f
) for the Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}
is equivalent to the following evolution equation for the elastic stress tensor
\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}=\kappa(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I})
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}+\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}-2\kappa\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}+\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T},

(2.88a)

while, in the Giesekus model, (
2.42
) is equivalent to the following evolution equation for
\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}+\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}-2\kappa\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}+\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}.

(2.88b)

For more details concerning the calculation, we refer to
[
47
, eq. (205)]
for the Oldroyd-B model and to
[
47
, eq. (187)]
for the Giesekus model.

3
A viscoelastic tumour model with stress diffusion

In this section, we consider a variant of the system
, where we fix the mass density of the mixture as
\varrho\coloneqq 1
and we neglect local exchange of mass, i.e.
\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\coloneqq 0
, see (
2.58
).
From the modelling point of view, the term
\Gamma_{\varphi}
usually describes biological effects like proliferation or apoptosis of the tumour, whereas the term
\Gamma_{\sigma}
models nutrient consumption of the tumour
[
31
]
. Moreover, elastic stresses are supposed to influence growth.
Hence, it makes sense to assume
\Gamma_{\varphi}
to depend on
\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}
, and
\Gamma_{\sigma}
to depend on
\varphi,\sigma
, respectively.
Moreover, we choose the nutrient energy density (
2.63
) and assume that the elastic energy is hence given by
W(\mathbb{B})=\frac{1}{2}\kappa\trace\big(\mathbb{B}-\ln\mathbb{B}\big)
, which corresponds to (
2.65
) with the elasticity parameters
\kappa=\kappa_{0}
not depending on
\varphi
.
Besides, we assume small stress diffusion effects, i.e., we add the dissipative term
+\alpha\Delta\mathbb{B}

to the right-hand side of the Oldroyd-B equation, which improves the mathematical properties of the system. This mathematical regularization can physically be motivated from a nonlocal energy storage mechanism or a nonlocal entropy production mechanism, see, e.g.,
[
48
]
.

Then, the mathematical system of our interest reads:

Problem
:

For a given constant
\alpha>0
, consider the system in
\Omega\times(0,T)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla\varphi

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({m(\varphi)\nabla\mu}\right)+\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}),

(3.1a)

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=A\psi^{\prime}(\varphi)-B\Delta\varphi-\chi_{\varphi}\sigma,

(3.1b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma+\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla\sigma

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({n(\varphi)\nabla(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi)}\right)-\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma),

(3.1c)

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right)

\displaystyle=0,

(3.1d)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}-\operatorname{div}\left({2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\right)+\nabla p

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({\kappa(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I})}\right)+\mu\nabla\varphi+(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi)\nabla\sigma,

(3.1e)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi)}\big(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}\big)

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}+\alpha\Delta\mathbb{B},

(3.1f)

together with the boundary conditions on
\partial\Omega\times(0,T)

\displaystyle\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}

\displaystyle=\nabla\mu\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}=0,

(3.1g)

\displaystyle\chi_{\sigma}n(\varphi)\nabla\sigma\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}

\displaystyle=K(\sigma_{\infty}-\sigma),

(3.1h)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle=0,

(3.1i)

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}

\displaystyle=0,

(3.1j)

and the initial data
\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0}
,
\sigma(0)=\sigma_{0}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}(0)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0}
and
\mathbb{B}(0)=\mathbb{B}_{0}
.

3.1
Assumptions and existence of weak solutions

In this section, we state the definition of a weak solution of
and provide an existence result in two space dimensions. First, we state our assumptions.

Assumptions 3.1
.

(\mathrm{A}1)

Let
T>0
and suppose that
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\{2,3\}
, is a convex, polygonal domain with boundary
\partial\Omega
.

(\mathrm{A}2)

For
d\in\{2,3\}
, the source functions
\Gamma_{\varphi}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\Gamma_{\sigma}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are continuous and there exists a constant
R_{0}>0
such that, for all
\varphi,\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathbb{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B})}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)}\right\rvert\leq R_{0}(1+\left\lvert{\varphi}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{\sigma}\right\rvert).

(\mathrm{A}3)

Let
\chi_{\varphi}\geq 0
and
\chi_{\sigma},A,B,K,\kappa,\alpha>0
be constants. Moreover, let
m,n,\eta,\tau\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R})
and suppose there exist constants
m_{0},m_{1},n_{0},n_{1},\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\tau_{0},\tau_{1}>0
such that, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle m_{0}

\displaystyle\leq m(t)\leq m_{1},\quad n_{0}\leq n(t)\leq n_{1},\quad\eta_{0}\leq\eta(t)\leq\eta_{1},\quad\tau_{0}\leq\tau(t)\leq\tau_{1}.

(\mathrm{A}4)

The potential
\psi
is non-negative and belongs to
C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
with

\displaystyle\psi(t)

\displaystyle\geq R_{1}\left\lvert{t}\right\rvert^{2}-R_{2}\qquad\forall\ t\in\mathbb{R},

(3.2)

where
R_{1},R_{2}>0
. Additionally, the potential can be decomposed as
\psi=\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}
with
\psi_{1}
convex and
\psi_{2}
concave such that

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\psi_{i}^{\prime}(t)}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq R_{3}(1+\left\lvert{t}\right\rvert)\qquad\forall\ t\in\mathbb{R},

(3.3)

where
i=1,2
and
R_{3}>0
. Moreover, we assume

\displaystyle A>\frac{4\chi_{\varphi}^{2}}{\chi_{\sigma}R_{1}}.

(3.4)

(\mathrm{A}5)

For the initial and boundary data, assume

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{N}}(\Omega)\coloneqq\{q\in H^{2}(\Omega)\mid\nabla q\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega\},\quad\sigma_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega),\quad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0}\in\mathbf{H},\quad\sigma_{\infty}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega)),

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{SPD}})\quad\text{with}\quad b^{0}_{\min}\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\rvert^{2}\leq\boldsymbol{\xi}^{T}\mathbb{B}_{0}(x)\boldsymbol{\xi}\leq b^{0}_{\max}\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\rvert^{2}\quad\forall\ \boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\Omega,

where
d\in\{2,3\}
,
b^{0}_{\min},b^{0}_{\max}\in\mathbb{R}
with
0<b^{0}_{\min}\leq b^{0}_{\max}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{n}}
denotes the outer unit normal on
{\partial\Omega}
.

(\mathrm{A}6)

The spatial dimension is restricted to
d=2
.

(\mathrm{A}7)

The source functions
\Gamma_{\varphi},\Gamma_{\sigma}
from
are Lipschitz continuous.

Unter these assumptions, we provide an existence result for weak solutions to
.
However, note that
–
are stated for arbitrary dimensions
d\in\{2,3\}
. This is sufficient when studying stability and existence of discrete solutions to a fully-discrete finite element approximation of
in Section
4
. Later, also
is needed to improve the regularity of discrete solutions. Moreover,
is needed for the limit passing in the discrete scheme in presence of mass lumping but can be dropped if the terms containing
\Gamma_{\varphi},\Gamma_{\sigma}
are integrated exactly.

Possible choices for the source functions which fulfill the assumptions can be constructed as follows. Let

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B})\coloneqq h(\varphi)\big(\mathcal{P}g(\sigma)f(\mathbb{B})-\mathcal{A}\big),\qquad\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)\coloneqq\mathcal{C}h(\varphi)g(\sigma),

(3.5)

where
\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C}\geq 0
are non-negative constants accounting for proliferation, apoptosis and nutrient consumption, and

\displaystyle h(\varphi)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\left\{0,\ \min\left\{\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\varphi),1\right\}\right\}\qquad

\displaystyle\forall\ \varphi\in\mathbb{R},

(3.6a)

\displaystyle g(\sigma)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\left\{0,\ \min\left\{\sigma,1\right\}\right\}\qquad

\displaystyle\forall\ \sigma\in\mathbb{R},

(3.6b)

\displaystyle f(\mathbb{B})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(1+\left\lvert{\kappa(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I})}\right\rvert^{2}\right)^{-1/2}\qquad

\displaystyle\forall\ \ \mathbb{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}.

(3.6c)

Then, as
f,g,h
are non-negative, Lipschitz-continuous and bounded, the source functions
\Gamma_{\varphi},\Gamma_{\sigma}
satisfy the assumptions from above, i.e.
and
.
The mobility functions
m,n
, the viscosity
\eta
and the relaxation time
\tau
can be defined with similar cut-offs outside of the interval
[-1,1]
such that
holds.

In practice, the polynomial double-well potential
\tilde{\psi}(t)=\tfrac{1}{4}(1-t^{2})^{2}
is a common choice. However, in order to fulfill
, the growth of the polynomial double-well potential shall be restricted to be at most quadratic for, e.g.,
t\not\in[-1,1]
, i.e.

\displaystyle\psi(t)=\begin{cases}t^{2}-2t+1&\mbox{if }t>1,\\
\frac{1}{4}(1-t^{2})^{2}&\mbox{if }t\in[-1,1],\\
t^{2}+2t+1&\mbox{if }t<-1,\end{cases}\qquad\psi^{\prime}(t)=\begin{cases}2t-2&\mbox{if }t>1,\\
t^{3}-t&\mbox{if }t\in[-1,1],\\
2t+2&\mbox{if }t<-1.\end{cases}

(3.7)

Besides, the parameter
A
is often chosen as
A=\frac{\beta}{\epsilon}
with
\beta>0
and a small constant
\epsilon>0
relating to the thickness of the diffuse interface.
Therefore, (
3.4
) is not a severe constraint.

Definition 3.2

(Weak solution)
.

Under the assumptions
–
, the quintuple
(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})
for
d=2
is called a weak solution of
if

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1})\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{2})\cap H^{1}(0,T;(H^{1})^{\prime}),

(3.8a)

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}),

(3.8b)

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2})\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})\cap H^{1}(0,T;(H^{1})^{\prime}),

(3.8c)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbf{H})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathbf{V})\cap W^{1,\frac{4}{3}}(0,T;\mathbf{V}^{\prime}),

(3.8d)

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}\left(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{SPD}})\right)\cap L^{2}\left(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{S}})\right)\cap W^{1,\frac{4}{3}}\left(0,T;(H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{S}}))^{\prime}\right),

(3.8e)

such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0}\text{ in }L^{2}(\Omega),\quad\sigma(0)=\sigma_{0}\text{ in }L^{2}(\Omega),\quad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}(0)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0}\text{ in }\mathbf{H},\\
&\mathbb{B}(0)=\mathbb{B}_{0}\text{ in }L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{S}})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{B}\text{ positive definite a.e.\penalty\ in }\Omega\times(0,T),\end{split}

(3.9)

and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left<{\partial_{t}\varphi,\zeta}\right>_{H^{1}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{\Omega_{T}}m(\varphi)\nabla\mu\cdot\nabla\zeta-\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B})\zeta-\varphi\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla\zeta\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.10a)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{T}}\mu\varrho-A\psi^{\prime}(\varphi)\varrho-B\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varrho+\chi_{\varphi}\sigma\varrho\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.10b)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left<{\partial_{t}\sigma,\xi}\right>_{H^{1}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{\Omega_{T}}n(\varphi)\nabla(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi)\cdot\nabla\xi+\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)\xi-\sigma\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla\xi\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\partial\Omega}K(\sigma-\sigma_{\infty})\xi\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.10c)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left<{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\right>_{\mathbf{V}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{\Omega_{T}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}+2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}):\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}})+\kappa(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}):\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}})\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Omega_{T}}\big(\varphi\nabla\mu+\sigma\nabla(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi)\big)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.10d)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left<{\partial_{t}\mathbb{B},\mathbb{C}}\right>_{H^{1}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{\Omega_{T}}\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi)}(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}):\mathbb{C}-2(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}):\mathbb{C}+\alpha\nabla\mathbb{B}:\nabla\mathbb{C}-\mathbb{B}:(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{C}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.10e)

for all
\zeta,\varrho,\xi\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}\in L^{4}(0,T;\mathbf{V})
and
\mathbb{C}\in L^{4}\left(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\mathrm{S}})\right)
.

Theorem 3.3

(Existence of weak solutions)
.

Let
–
hold. Then, there exists a weak solution
(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})
of
in the sense of Definition
3.2
. Moreover, there exist positive constants
C_{1}(T),C_{2}(T,\alpha^{-1})
, both depending exponentially on
T
and
C_{2}(T,\alpha^{-1})
depending additionally on
\alpha^{-1}
, such that

\displaystyle\left\|{\varphi}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1})}+\left\|{\varphi}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\varphi}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;(H^{1})^{\prime})}+\left\|{\mu}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2})}+\left\|{\sigma}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\sigma}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;(H^{1})^{\prime})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2})}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}\leq C_{1}(T),

(3.11a)

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\|_{L^{4/3}(0,T;\mathbf{V}^{\prime})}+\left\|{\mathbb{B}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2})}+\left\|{\mathbb{B}}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}}\right\|_{L^{4/3}(0,T;(H^{1})^{\prime})}\leq C_{2}(T,\alpha^{-1}).

(3.11b)

Remark 3.4
.

(i)

This existence result will be proved in Section
4
by the passage to the limit in a fully-discrete finite element scheme in two dimensions, where a CFL condition is necessary, i.e.
\Delta t\leq Ch^{2}
with a possibly very small positive constant
C
, see Theorem
4.18
. Further, the additional Lipschitz assumption
on the source terms is needed for the limit passing in presence of mass lumping, but it can be dropped if the integrals containing
\Gamma_{\varphi},\Gamma_{\sigma}
are evaluated exactly.

(ii)

As
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbf{V})
, integration by parts over
\Omega
in the convection term in (
3.10a
) leads to (
3.10a
) with
-\varphi\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla\zeta
replaced by
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla\varphi\zeta
, which is consistent with (
3.1a
). One can argue similarly for the convection terms in (
3.10
), (
3.10e
) and the last term in (
3.10
).

(iii)

Moreover, one can obtain (
3.10e
) with
2\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}
replaced by
\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}
, which is consistent with (
3.1f
), by choosing the test function
\mathbb{C}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{G}+\mathbb{G}^{T})
, where
\mathbb{G}\in L^{4}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}))
, and using the symmetry of
\mathbb{B}
.

(iv)

This existence result still holds true if source or sink terms for
\mathbb{B}
are included like in (
2.71
), i.e. if (
3.1f
) is replaced by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}+\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi)}\big(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}\big)

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}-\gamma(\varphi,\sigma)\mathbb{B}+\alpha\Delta\mathbb{B},

(3.12)

where
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous and bounded. The additional term
\gamma(\varphi,\sigma)\mathbb{B}
on the right-hand side can be controlled with a Gronwall argument similarly to (
3.21
).

3.2
Formal a priori estimates

To better understand the strategy for the proof of Theorem
3.3
, we temporarily assume that
–
hold and that
(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,p,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})
is a sufficiently smooth solution of
with
\mathbb{B}
positive definite in
\Omega_{T}\coloneqq\Omega\times(0,T)
. The first step is to provide the formal derivation of
a priori
estimates based on the energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\varphi,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}A\psi(\varphi)+\frac{B}{2}\left\lvert{\nabla\varphi}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\chi_{\sigma}}{2}\left\lvert{\sigma}\right\rvert^{2}+\chi_{\varphi}\sigma(1-\varphi)+\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\trace(\mathbb{B}-\ln\mathbb{B})\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

(3.13)

Note that this energy is not finite if
\mathbb{B}
is not positive definite, which is due to the logarithmic term.
Later, with the help of suitable regularization techniques, it turns out that the positive definiteness of the left Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}
is preserved for all
t>0
if
\mathbb{B}(t=0)
is positive definite. This has also been observed for other viscoelastic systems in the literature,
see, e.g.,
[
39
, Lem. 2.1]
or
[
46
, Rem. 3.4]
.

Moreover, the energy
\mathcal{F}(\varphi,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})
can become negative due to the term
\sigma(1-\varphi)
. This is one reason why the derivation of reasonable
a priori
estimates must be performed carefully.

From the general energy identity (
2.50
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\mathcal{F}(\varphi,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})+\int_{\Omega}m(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla\mu}\right\rvert^{2}+n(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi)}\right\rvert^{2}+2\eta(\varphi)\left\lvert{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\quad+\int_{\partial\Omega}K\chi_{\sigma}\left\lvert{\sigma}\right\rvert^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\int_{\Omega}\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2\tau(\varphi)}\trace\big(\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)-\frac{\alpha\kappa}{2}\nabla\mathbb{B}:\nabla\mathbb{B}^{-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\mu\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B})-(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi))\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}K(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi))\sigma_{\infty}-K\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi)\sigma\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}.\end{split}

(3.14)

We recall that this is obtained by formally multiplying (
3.1a
) with
\mu
, (
3.1b
) with
\partial_{t}\varphi
, (
3.1c
) with
\chi_{\sigma}\sigma+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi)
, (
3.1e
) with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
and (
3.1f
) with
\frac{\kappa}{2}(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{-1})
, integrating over the domain
\Omega
, using Green’s formula and then summing up the resulting equations.

Apart form the energy, the terms on the left-hand side of (
3.14
) are non-negative as
\mathbb{B}
is positive definite and as the functions
m(\cdot),n(\cdot),\eta(\cdot),\tau(\cdot)
are continuous, uniformly positive and bounded due to
. As
\mathbb{B}
is symmetric positive definite, we note that it holds

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{B}:\nabla\mathbb{B}^{-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{d}\left\lvert{\nabla\trace(\ln\mathbb{B})}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,\quad\text{ and }\quad\trace\big(\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)=\left\lvert{(\mathbb{I}-\mathbb{B}^{-1})\sqrt{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rvert^{2}\geq 0,

see
[
9
, Lem. 3.1]
for the first inequality.

Now we estimate the terms on the right-hand side of (
3.14
). First, for the terms involving the boundary integrals on the right-hand side of (
3.14
), we apply Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities and the trace theorem to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\lvert{\int_{\partial\Omega}K(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi))\sigma_{\infty}-K\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi)\sigma\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\frac{3}{4}K\chi_{\sigma}\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}+C(K,C_{\mathrm{tr}},\chi_{\varphi},\chi_{\sigma})\|{\varphi}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+C(K,\chi_{\varphi},\chi_{\sigma})\big(\left\lvert{\partial\Omega}\right\rvert+\|{\sigma_{\infty}}\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}\big).\end{split}

(3.15)

The terms on the right-hand side of (
3.14
) involving the source terms
\Gamma_{\varphi}
and
\Gamma_{\sigma}
are bounded as follows,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\lvert{\int_{\Omega}\mu\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B})-\big(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi)\big)\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)\,{\mathrm{d}}x}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}\|{\mu}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C(R_{0},\chi_{\sigma},\chi_{\varphi})\Big(\|{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)+C(R_{0},\chi_{\varphi},\Omega),\end{split}

(3.16)

where we used
such as Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities.
However, one now needs an
L^{2}(\Omega)
-bound for the chemical potential in order to control the source terms.
This is obtained by multiplying (
3.1b
) with
\mu
and integrating over the domain
\Omega
and applying Green’s formula, which yields

\displaystyle\|{\mu}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\int_{\Omega}\big(A\psi^{\prime}(\varphi)-\chi_{\varphi}\sigma\big)\mu+B\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\mu\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

At this point, we also need that the elasticity parameter
\kappa
is independent of
\varphi
, otherwise we would have to control additional
\mathbb{B}
-dependent terms, see (
2.38b
), for which we do not have any
a priori
knowledge.

In absence of any
a priori
estimate for
\varphi
, we can control
\|{\mu}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
only if
\psi^{\prime}(\cdot)
has at most linear growth.
Hence, one obtains with Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities that

\displaystyle\|{\mu}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{m_{0}}{4}\|{\nabla\mu}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C(A,B,R_{3},\chi_{\varphi},m_{0})\Big(1+\|{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\nabla\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big).

(3.17)

Moreover, we use

\displaystyle\begin{split}\chi_{\sigma}^{2}\|{\nabla\sigma}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}&\leq 2\left\|{\chi_{\sigma}\nabla\sigma-\chi_{\varphi}\nabla\varphi}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\chi_{\varphi}^{2}\|{\nabla\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},\end{split}

(3.18)

such that (
3.14
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\mathcal{F}(\varphi,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})+C\Big(\|{\mu}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|{\nabla\sigma}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}\Big)\\
&\quad+C\Big(\|{\trace\big(\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)}\|_{L^{1}}+\alpha\|{\nabla\trace(\ln\mathbb{B})}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)\\
&\leq C\Big(1+\|{\varphi}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\sigma_{\infty}}\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}\Big).\end{split}

(3.19)

As the term
\chi_{\varphi}\sigma(1-\varphi)
in the energy can have a negative sign, the next step is to absorb it with the help of the non-negative terms in the energy. In particular, we first apply Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\int_{\Omega}\chi_{\varphi}\sigma(1-\varphi)\,{\mathrm{d}}x}\right\rvert\leq\frac{\chi_{\sigma}}{4}\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{2\chi_{\varphi}^{2}}{\chi_{\sigma}}\|{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

so that we obtain by integrating over
t\in(0,s)
, where
s\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&A\|{\psi(\varphi(s))}\|_{L^{1}}+\frac{B}{2}\|{\nabla\varphi(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\chi_{\sigma}}{4}\|{\sigma(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\|{\trace(\mathbb{B}(s)-\ln\mathbb{B}(s))}\|_{L^{1}}\\
&\quad+C\Big(\|{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;H^{1})}^{2}+\|{\nabla\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\|{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2}(\partial\Omega))}^{2}\Big)\\
&\quad+C\Big(\|{\trace\big(\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)}\|_{L^{1}(0,s;L^{1})}+\alpha\|{\nabla\trace(\ln\mathbb{B})}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}\Big)\\
&\leq\frac{2\chi_{\varphi}^{2}}{\chi_{\sigma}}\|{\varphi(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\Big(1+\left\lvert{\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0},\sigma_{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0})}\right\rvert+\|{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;H^{1})}^{2}+\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\|{\sigma_{\infty}}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2}(\partial\Omega))}^{2}\Big).\end{split}

(3.20)

Then, by (
3.2
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left(AR_{1}-\frac{2\chi_{\varphi}^{2}}{\chi_{\sigma}}\right)\|{\varphi(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{B}{2}\|{\nabla\varphi(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\chi_{\sigma}}{4}\|{\sigma(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\|{\trace(\mathbb{B}(s)-\ln\mathbb{B}(s))}\|_{L^{1}}\\
&\quad+C\Big(\|{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;H^{1})}^{2}+\|{\nabla\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\|{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2}(\partial\Omega))}^{2}\Big)\\
&\quad+C\Big(\|{\trace\big(\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)}\|_{L^{1}(0,s;L^{1})}+\alpha\|{\nabla\trace(\ln\mathbb{B})}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}\Big)\\
&\leq C\Big(1+\left\lvert{\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0},\sigma_{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0})}\right\rvert+\|{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;H^{1})}^{2}+\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\|{\sigma_{\infty}}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2}(\partial\Omega))}^{2}\Big).\end{split}

(3.21)

Note that
AR_{1}-\frac{2\chi_{\varphi}^{2}}{\chi_{\sigma}}
is positive due to (
3.4
), which is not a severe constraint in practice, as
A=\frac{\beta}{\epsilon}
with a small
\epsilon>0
. Hence, we apply a Gronwall argument (see below for Lemma
3.5
), to obtain the inequality

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|{\varphi(s)}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|{\sigma(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\trace(\mathbb{B}(s)-\ln\mathbb{B}(s))}\|_{L^{1}}\\
&\quad+\|{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;H^{1})}^{2}+\|{\nabla\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\|{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2}(\partial\Omega))}^{2}\\
&\quad+\|{\trace\big(\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{B}^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)}\|_{L^{1}(0,s;L^{1})}+\alpha\|{\nabla\trace(\ln\mathbb{B})}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}\\
&\leq C\Big(1+\left\lvert{\mathcal{F}(\varphi_{0},\sigma_{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0})}\right\rvert+\|{\sigma_{\infty}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\partial\Omega))}^{2}\Big),\end{split}

(3.22)

for almost all
s\in(0,T)
. This leads to formal
a priori
estimates as the right-hand side of (
3.22
) is bounded due to
.

For completeness, we recall the following Gronwall inequality from
[
29
, Lem. 3.1]
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,u
and v be real-valued functions defined on
I=[0,T]
. Assume that
\alpha
is integrable,
\beta
is non-negative and continuous,
u
is continuous,
v
is non-negative and continuous. Suppose
u
and
v
satisfy the integral inequality

\displaystyle u(s)+\int_{0}^{s}v(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq\alpha(s)+\int_{0}^{s}\beta(t)u(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\quad\forall\ s\in I.

Then it follows

\displaystyle u(s)+\int_{0}^{s}v(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq\alpha(s)+\int_{0}^{s}\alpha(t)\beta(t)\exp\Big(\int_{t}^{s}\beta(r)\,{\mathrm{d}}r\Big)\,{\mathrm{d}}t.

3.3
Stronger bounds in two spatial dimensions

The bounds on
\mathbb{B}
are not sufficiently strong to establish existence of a solution. However, we get an estimate in a stronger norm if we restrict to two spatial dimensions. Hence, suppose that
holds true in addition to
–
.
To derive higher order estimates for
\mathbb{B}
, we formally multiply (
3.1f
) with
\mathbb{B}
, integrate over
\Omega
and apply Green’s formula to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathbb{B}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi)}\|{\mathbb{B}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\|{\nabla\mathbb{B}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi)}\trace\mathbb{B}+2\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}:\mathbb{B}^{2}-(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{B}:\mathbb{B}\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

(3.23)

On noting (
3.1d
), the last term in (
3.23
) vanishes by integration by parts.
Then, with Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities and a Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation inequality (see, e.g.,
[
6
]
) for
d=2
, it holds

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\|{\mathbb{B}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\|{\mathbb{B}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\alpha\|{\nabla\mathbb{B}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C(\alpha^{-1})\big(1+\|{\mathbb{B}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\|{\mathbb{B}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\big),

(3.24)

where
C(\alpha^{-1})
denotes a constant that depends on the inverse of the viscoelastic diffusion parameter
\alpha
.
It follows with integration in time and Lemma
3.5
that

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{B}(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\mathbb{B}}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\alpha\|{\nabla\mathbb{B}}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}\leq C\big(\alpha^{-1},\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\big)\|{\mathbb{B}_{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(3.25)

for almost all
s\in(0,T)
. The right-hand side of (
3.25
) is bounded due to (
3.22
) and
.

3.4
Formal estimates for a regularized problem

Showing the positive definiteness of the left Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}
is one of the main difficulties we have to deal with.
Here, we apply a regularization strategy of Barrett and Boyaval
[
6
]
and introduce a regularized problem with a cut-off on the left Cauchy–Green tensor on certain terms in the system
.

First, we introduce the following concave regularized approximations of the logarithm function
G(s)=\ln(s)
and of the identity
\beta(s)=[G^{\prime}(s)]^{-1}=s
for all
s>0
similarly to
[
6
, Sec. 2.1]
:

\displaystyle G_{\delta}(s)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{s}{\delta}+\ln(\delta)-1,&s<\delta,\\
\ln(s),&s\geq\delta,\end{cases}\quad\quad\quad

\displaystyle\beta_{\delta}(s)

\displaystyle=\big[G_{\delta}^{\prime}(s)\big]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\max\{s,\delta\}\quad\forall\ s\in\mathbb{R},

(3.26)

\displaystyle G^{L}(s)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\ln(s),&s\in(0,L),\\
\frac{s}{L}+\ln(L)-1,&s\geq L,\end{cases}\quad\quad\quad

\displaystyle\beta^{L}(s)

\displaystyle=\big[G^{L^{\prime}}(s)\big]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\min\{s,L\}\quad\forall\ s>0,

(3.27)

where
0<\delta<1<L
, also see Figure
3
.
We also define the concave
C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
function

\displaystyle H_{\delta}(s)\coloneqq G^{\delta^{-1}}(s)\quad\forall\ s\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}.

(3.28)

\delta
1
L
s
G(s)
G^{L}(s)
G_{\delta}(s)

(a)

\delta
1
L
s
\beta(s)
\beta^{L}(s)
\beta_{\delta}(s)

(b)

Figure 3:
The functions
G
(left) and
\beta
(right) and their regularizations.

We recall the following result from
[
6
, Lem. 2.1]
. Let us note that the domain of definition of scalar functions is naturally extended to symmetric matrices in terms of the eigenvalues.

Lemma 3.6
.

For all
\Phi,\Psi\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{S}}
and for any
\delta\in(0,1)
, it holds

\displaystyle\beta_{\delta}(\Phi)G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\Phi)\beta_{\delta}(\Phi)=\mathbb{I},

(3.29a)

\displaystyle\trace\big(\beta_{\delta}(\Phi)+[\beta_{\delta}(\Phi)]^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)

\displaystyle\geq 0,

(3.29b)

\displaystyle\trace\big(\Phi-G_{\delta}(\Phi)-\mathbb{I}\big)

\displaystyle\geq 0,

(3.29c)

\displaystyle\big(\Phi-\beta_{\delta}(\Phi)\big):\big(\mathbb{I}-G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\Phi)\big)

\displaystyle\geq 0,

(3.29d)

\displaystyle(\Phi-\Psi):G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\Psi)

\displaystyle\geq\trace\big(G_{\delta}(\Phi)-G_{\delta}(\Psi)\big),

(3.29e)

\displaystyle-(\Phi-\Psi):\big(G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\Phi)-G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\Psi)\big)

\displaystyle\geq\delta^{2}\left\lvert{G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\Phi)-G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\Psi)}\right\rvert^{2}.

(3.29f)

In addition, if
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
, it holds

\displaystyle\trace\big(\Phi-G_{\delta}(\Phi)\big)

\displaystyle\geq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{\Phi}\right\rvert,\\
\frac{1}{2\delta}\left\lvert{[\Phi]_{-}}\right\rvert,\end{cases}

(3.29g)

\displaystyle\Phi:\big(\mathbb{I}-G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\Phi)\big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{\Phi}\right\rvert-d,

(3.29h)

where
[\cdot]_{-}
denotes the negative part function defined by
[s]_{-}\coloneqq\min\{s,0\}

\forall\ s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Let us return to the problem
and introduce the reguralized problem with a cut-off on the left Cauchy–Green tensor on certain terms in the system.

Problem
:

Let
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
. The regularized problem
corresponds to
with (
3.1e
)–(
3.1f
) replaced by

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}-\operatorname{div}\left({2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\right)+\nabla p

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}\left({\kappa(\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B})-\mathbb{I})}\right)+\mu\nabla\varphi+(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi)\nabla\sigma,

(3.30)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}+(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\cdot\nabla)\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B})+\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi)}\big(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}\big)

\displaystyle=\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B})+\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B})(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})^{T}+\alpha\Delta\mathbb{B}.

(3.31)

Now again, we temporarily assume that
–
hold and that
(\varphi,\mu,\sigma,p,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})
is a sufficiently smooth solution of
for a given
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
. The first step is again to provide the formal derivation of
a priori
estimates based on the regularized energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(\varphi,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}A\psi(\varphi)+\frac{B}{2}\left\lvert{\nabla\varphi}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\chi_{\sigma}}{2}\left\lvert{\sigma}\right\rvert^{2}+\chi_{\varphi}\sigma(1-\varphi)+\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\trace(\mathbb{B}-G_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}))\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

(3.32)

Let us remark that
\mathbb{B}
does not necessarily have to be positive definite in presence of the regularization parameter
\delta
as the term
\trace(\mathbb{B}-G_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}))
is well-defined even if
\mathbb{B}
is not positive definite.

We perform a similar testing procedure as in (
3.14
). More concretely, we formally multiply (
3.1a
) with
\mu
, (
3.1b
) with
\partial_{t}\varphi
, (
3.1c
) with
\chi_{\sigma}\sigma+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi)
, (
3.30
) with
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
and (
3.31
) with
\frac{\kappa}{2}(\mathbb{I}-G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}))
, integrate over the domain
\Omega
, apply Green’s formula, and then sum up the resulting equations, so that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(\varphi,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B})+\int_{\Omega}m(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla\mu}\right\rvert^{2}+n(\varphi)\left\lvert{\nabla(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi)}\right\rvert^{2}+2\eta(\varphi)\left\lvert{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\quad+\int_{\partial\Omega}K\chi_{\sigma}\left\lvert{\sigma}\right\rvert^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\int_{\Omega}\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2\tau(\varphi)}(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}):(\mathbb{I}-G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}))-\frac{\alpha\kappa}{2}\nabla\mathbb{B}:\nabla G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}))\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\mu\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B})-(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi))\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}K(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi))\sigma_{\infty}-K\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi)\sigma\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}.\end{split}

(3.33)

On noting (
3.29a
) and (
3.29d
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I}):\big(\mathbb{I}-G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B})\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega}\trace\big(\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B})+[\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B})]^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)\,{\mathrm{d}}x\geq 0,

(3.34)

and similarly to (
3.29f
), see
[
6
, Sec. 4.2]
, it holds

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{B}:\nabla G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B})\,{\mathrm{d}}x\geq\delta^{2}\int_{\Omega}\left\lvert{\nabla G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B})}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

(3.35)

Then, with arguments that are similar to (
3.22
), the following inequality can be derived, for almost all
s\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|{\varphi(s)}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|{\sigma(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}(s)}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\trace(\mathbb{B}(s)-G_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}(s))}\|_{L^{1}}\\
&\quad+\|{\mu}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;H^{1})}^{2}+\|{\nabla\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\|{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}})}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}+\|{\sigma}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2}(\partial\Omega))}^{2}\\
&\quad+\left\|{\trace\big(\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B})+[\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B})]^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)}\right\|_{L^{1}(0,s;L^{1})}+\alpha\delta^{2}\|{\nabla G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B})}\|_{L^{2}(0,s;L^{2})}^{2}\\
&\leq C\Big(1+\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(\varphi_{0},\sigma_{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0},\mathbb{B}_{0})+\|{\sigma_{\infty}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\partial\Omega))}^{2}\Big),\end{split}

(3.36)

which holds uniformly in
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Moreover, with (
3.29g
), it additionally holds

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{B}(s)}\|_{L^{1}}+\frac{1}{\delta}\left\|{[\mathbb{B}(s)]_{-}}\right\|_{L^{1}}\leq\|{\trace(\mathbb{B}(s)-G_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}(s))}\|_{L^{1}},

(3.37)

for almost all
s\in(0,T)
, which, together with (
3.36
), makes sure that the eigenvalues of
\mathbb{B}
are positive in the formal limit
\delta\to 0
.

4
Finite element approximation of the model with stress diffusion

In this section, we provide a proof for Theorem
3.3
with the following strategy.
First, we attend some ideas of
[
6
, Sec. 5]
and introduce a finite element approximation of the problem
, which helps us to mimic the inequality (
3.36
) on the fully discrete level (see Section
4.3
) and to show that there exist stable
\delta
-regularized discrete solutions in abritrary dimensions
d\in\{2,3\}
, see Section
4.4
.
In Section
4.5
, we pass to the limit
\delta\to 0
and obtain the existence of discrete functions for
d\in\{2,3\}
, including a positive definite discrete left Cauchy–Green tensor, which solve a finite element approximation of the problem
.
After that, we first improve the regularity of discrete solutions in arbitrary dimensions
d\in\{2,3\}
in Section
4.6
and then restrict to
d=2
to improve the regularity of the discrete Cauchy–Green tensor and the discrete velocity in Section
4.7
.
Finally, in Section
4.8
, we send the discretization parameters to zero in order to obtain existence of a global-in-time weak solution to the problem
in two dimensions.

Let us introduce the notation for the fully-discrete finite element approximation.
From now on, we throughout assume that
holds, i.e., suppose that
T>0
and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\{2,3\}
, is a convex, polygonal domain with boundary
\partial\Omega
. We split the time interval
[0,T)
into intervals
[t^{n-1},t^{n})
with
t^{n}=n\Delta t
and
t^{N_{T}}=T
, where
\Delta t>0
and
n=0,...,N_{T}
.
We require
\{\mathcal{T}_{h}\}_{h>0}
to be a quasi-uniform family of conforming triangulations with mesh parameter
h>0
(in the sense of
[
12
]
). We also require that the family of meshes
\{\mathcal{T}_{h}\}_{h>0}
consists only of non-obtuse simplices.
For a given partitioning of meshes
\mathcal{T}_{h}
, we denote the simplices by
K_{k}
with
k\in\{1,...,N_{K}\}
. The set of internal edges of triangles (
d=2
) in the mesh
\mathcal{T}_{h}
or facets of tetrahedra (
d=3
) is denoted by
\partial\mathcal{T}_{h}=\{E_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N_{E}}
. The set of all the vertices of
\mathcal{T}_{h}
is denoted by
\{P_{p}\}_{p=1}^{N_{p}}
.

Let us consider the problem
.
We approximate the scalar variables
\varphi
,
\mu
and
\sigma
and the matrix valued quantity
\mathbb{B}
with continuous and piecewise linear functions.
Hence, we define the following scalar
\mathcal{P}_{1}
-finite element space

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{q_{h}\in C(\overline{\Omega})\mid q_{h}|_{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(K)\ \forall\ K\in\mathcal{T}_{h}\right\}\subset H^{1}(\Omega),

(4.1a)

and the matrix valued
\mathcal{P}_{1}
-finite element space

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\mathbb{B}_{h}\in C(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{S}})\mid\mathbb{B}_{h}|_{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(K;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{S}})\ \forall\ K\in\mathcal{T}_{h}\right\}\subset H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{S}}).

(4.1b)

Moreover, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{h,\mathrm{PD}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\mathbb{B}_{h}\in\mathcal{W}_{h}\mid\mathbb{B}_{h}(P_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{SPD}}\ \forall\ p=1,...,N_{p}\right\}.

(4.1c)

For the velocity vector
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
and the pressure
p
, we use the
\mathcal{P}_{2}
–
\mathcal{P}_{1}
-Taylor–Hood element
[
34
]
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}\in C(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap H^{1}_{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\mid\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}|_{K}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(K;\mathbb{R}^{d})\ \forall\ K\in\mathcal{T}_{h}\right\},

(4.1d)

for the discrete velocity and
\mathcal{S}_{h}\cap L^{2}_{0}(\Omega)
for the discrete pressure.
We also introduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{h,\mathrm{div}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{h}\mid\int_{\Omega}\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}}\right)q_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=0\ \forall\ q_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}\right\},

(4.1e)

which approximates the space
\mathbf{V}
.

It is well-known (cf.
[
34
]
) that this choice for the discrete velocity–pressure space satisfies the discrete Ladyzhenskaya–Babuška–Brezzi (LBB) stability condition

\displaystyle\inf_{q_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}}\sup_{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{h}}\frac{\int_{\Omega}\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}}\right)q_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x}{\|{q_{h}}\|_{L^{2}}\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}}\|_{H^{1}}}\geq C>0,

(4.2)

where, unless otherwise stated,
C>0
always denotes a generic constant which is independent of
h,\Delta t,\alpha,\delta
.
At this point, let us mention that also other choices for the discrete velocity–pressure space can be used instead of the
\mathcal{P}_{2}
–
\mathcal{P}_{1}
-Taylor–Hood element as long as the discrete LBB stability condition (
4.2
) is fulfilled. For example, the mini-element
[
34
]
is also a suitable choice.

Moreover, we denote the standard nodal interpolation operator by
\mathcal{I}_{h}:C(\overline{\Omega})\to\mathcal{S}_{h}
such that
(\mathcal{I}_{h}\eta)(P_{p})=\eta(P_{p})
for all
p\in\{1,...,N_{p}\}
and
\eta\in C(\overline{\Omega})
, which is naturally extended to
\mathcal{I}_{h}:C(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\mathrm{S}})\to\mathcal{W}_{h}
.
As we use
mass lumping
, we introduce the following semi-inner products and the induced semi-norms on
C(\overline{\Omega})
and
C(\partial\Omega)
, respectively, by

\displaystyle\left({\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}\right)_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x,\quad\quad

\displaystyle\|{\eta_{1}}\|_{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sqrt{\left({\eta_{1},\eta_{1}}\right)_{h}},\quad\quad

\displaystyle\forall\ \eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in C(\overline{\Omega}),

(4.3)

\displaystyle\left({\eta_{3},\eta_{4}}\right)_{h,\partial\Omega}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\partial\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\eta_{3}\eta_{4}\big]\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1},\quad\quad

\displaystyle\|{\eta_{3}}\|_{h,{\partial\Omega}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sqrt{\left({\eta_{3},\eta_{3}}\right)_{h,{\partial\Omega}}}\quad\quad

\displaystyle\forall\ \eta_{3},\eta_{4}\in C({\partial\Omega}).

(4.4)

Below, we state some well-known properties concerning
\mathcal{S}_{h}
and the interpolant
\mathcal{I}_{h}
. Let
K\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
,
0\leq s\leq m\leq 1
and
1\leq r\leq p\leq\infty
. Then, as the family of triangulations is quasi-uniform, it holds for all
\eta\in H^{2}(\Omega)
and all
q_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
that

\displaystyle\|{\eta-\mathcal{I}_{h}\eta}\|_{L^{2}}+h\|{\nabla(\eta-\mathcal{I}_{h}\eta)}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}\left\lvert{\eta}\right\rvert_{H^{2}},

(4.5)

\displaystyle\left\lvert{q_{h}}\right\rvert_{W^{m,p}(K)}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{s-m+\frac{d}{p}-\frac{d}{r}}\left\lvert{q_{h}}\right\rvert_{W^{s,r}(K)},

(4.6)

see, e.g.,
[
12
, Thm. 3.3]
and
[
14
, Lem. 4.5.3]
, respectively.
It follows from an
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
-error estimate for
\mathcal{I}_{h}
(see
[
14
, Thm. 4.4.20]
) and an approximation argument by the Stone–Weierstrass theorem, that

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\|{\eta-\mathcal{I}_{h}\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=0\quad\quad\forall\ \eta\in C(\overline{\Omega}).

(4.7)

As the basis functions associated with
\mathcal{S}_{h}
are non-negative and sum to one everywhere, it follows from a Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, that

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\mathcal{I}_{h}\eta(x)}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\left\lvert{\eta(x)}\right\rvert^{2}\big]\quad\quad\forall\ x\in K,\ K\in\mathcal{T}_{h},\ \eta\in C(\overline{\Omega}).

(4.8)

We deduce from (
4.8
), (
4.6
) and Hölder’s inequality, that, for all
q_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
,

\displaystyle\|{q_{h}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|{q_{h}}\|_{h}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|{q_{h}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

(4.9)

\displaystyle\|{q_{h}}\|_{L^{2}({\partial\Omega})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|{q_{h}}\|_{h,{\partial\Omega}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|{q_{h}}\|_{L^{2}({\partial\Omega})}^{2}.

(4.10)

Applying (
4.5
) elementwise and then summing over all simplices yields the
mass lumping
error estimate

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\left({q_{h},\zeta_{h}}\right)_{h}-\left({q_{h},\zeta_{h}}\right)_{L^{2}}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}\|{\nabla q_{h}}\|_{L^{2}}\|{\nabla\zeta_{h}}\|_{L^{2}}\quad\quad\forall\ q_{h},\zeta_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}.

(4.11)

Now, we provide a technical result concerning the
mass lumping
errors on the boundary
{\partial\Omega}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
q_{h},\zeta_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
. Then, as
\{\mathcal{T}_{h}\}_{h>0}
is a conforming family of quasi-uniform partitionings, it holds

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\left({q_{h},\zeta_{h}}\right)_{h,{\partial\Omega}}-\left({q_{h},\zeta_{h}}\right)_{L^{2}({\partial\Omega})}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq Ch\|{\nabla q_{h}}\|_{L^{2}}\|{\nabla\zeta_{h}}\|_{L^{2}}.

(4.12)

Let
E\in\partial\mathcal{T}_{h}
be a side simplex with diameter
h_{E}
and let
K_{E}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
such that
E\subset\partial K_{E}\cap{\partial\Omega}
. Then, it holds with Hölder’s inequality and a local trace inequality (i.e.
[
12
, Lem. 4.2]
), that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lvert{\int_{E}(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I})[q_{h}\zeta_{h}]\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}}\right\rvert&\leq C\left\lvert{E}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\|{(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I})[q_{h}\zeta_{h}]}\|_{L^{2}(E)}\\
&\leq C\left\lvert{E}\right\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(h_{E}^{-1}\|{(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I})[q_{h}\zeta_{h}]}\|_{L^{2}(K_{E})}^{2}+h_{E}\|{\nabla(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I})[q_{h}\zeta_{h}]}\|_{L^{2}(K_{E})}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}},\end{split}

which gives us, on noting (
4.5
) and as the family of triangulations is quasi-uniform, that

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\int_{E}(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I})[q_{h}\zeta_{h}]\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\left\lvert{q_{h}\zeta_{h}}\right\rvert_{H^{2}(K_{E})}.

As
q_{h},\zeta_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
, we obtain with a product rule and with (
4.6
), that

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\int_{E}(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I})[q_{h}\zeta_{h}]\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{\frac{d}{2}+1}\|{\nabla q_{h}}\|_{L^{\infty}(K_{E})}\|{\nabla\zeta_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(K_{E})}\leq Ch\|{\nabla q_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(K_{E})}\|{\nabla\zeta_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(K_{E})}.

Summing over all
E\in\partial\mathcal{T}_{h}
with
E\subset{\partial\Omega}
and using the fact that each element
K_{E}
occurs at most
d+1
times imply (
4.12
).
∎

The results (
4.5
)–(
4.12
) can also be established with the corresponding matrix valued functions. The inverse inequality (
4.6
) also holds for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{h}
instead of
q_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
.

Furthermore, we recall the quasi-interpolation operator
\mathcal{I}_{h}^{\mathrm{Cl}}:L^{2}(\Omega)\to\mathcal{S}_{h}
from Clément
[
19
]
, which is defined by local averages instead of nodal values. The following properties are taken from
[
18
, Chap. 3]
:

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\eta-\mathcal{I}_{h}^{\mathrm{Cl}}\eta}\right\rvert_{W^{k,2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{m-k}\left\lvert{\eta}\right\rvert_{W^{m,2}}\quad

\displaystyle\forall\ \eta\in W^{m,2}(\Omega),\

\displaystyle 0\leq k\leq m\leq 2,

(4.13a)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{h\to 0}\|{\eta-\mathcal{I}_{h}^{\mathrm{Cl}}\eta}\|_{W^{k,2}}

\displaystyle=0,\quad

\displaystyle\forall\ \eta\in W^{k,2}(\Omega),\

\displaystyle 0\leq k\leq 1.

(4.13b)

Moreover, if only a finite number of patch shapes occur in the sequence of triangulations, then

\displaystyle\|{\eta-\mathcal{I}_{h}^{\mathrm{Cl}}\eta}\|_{L^{2}({\partial\Omega})}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{1/2}\|{\nabla\eta}\|_{L^{2}}\quad

\displaystyle\forall\ \eta\in H^{1}(\Omega),

(4.13c)

see
[
12
, Thm. 4.2]
. In practice, this assumption seems to be not that restrictive. Hence, we suppose it to hold.

4.1
Approximation of the initial and boundary values

In this work, we require the following assumptions for the discrete initial and boundary values.

Assumptions 4.2
.

Suppose that the discrete initial data
(\varphi_{h}^{0},\sigma_{h}^{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{0},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{0})\in(\mathcal{S}_{h})^{2}\times\mathcal{V}_{h,\mathrm{div}}\times\mathcal{W}_{h,\mathrm{PD}}
and discrete boundary data
\sigma_{\infty,h}\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{S}_{h})
fulfill the following bounds uniformly in
h,\Delta t,\alpha,\delta
:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\psi(\varphi_{h}^{0})\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\|{\varphi_{h}^{0}}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\Delta t\|{\Delta_{h}\varphi_{h}^{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C,

(4.14a)

\displaystyle\|{\sigma_{h}^{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\Delta t\|{\nabla\sigma_{h}^{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\Delta t\|{\sigma_{h}^{0}}\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C,

(4.14b)

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\Delta t\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C,

(4.14c)

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\Delta t\|{\nabla\mathbb{B}_{h}^{0}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C,

(4.14d)

\displaystyle\|{\sigma_{\infty,h}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\partial\Omega))}

\displaystyle\leq C,

(4.14e)

and, with constants
0<\tilde{b}^{0}_{\min}\leq\tilde{b}^{0}_{\max}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{b}^{0}_{\min}\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\rvert^{2}\leq\boldsymbol{\xi}^{T}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{0}(P_{p})\boldsymbol{\xi}

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{b}^{0}_{\max}\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\right\rvert^{2}\quad\quad\forall\ \boldsymbol{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \forall\ p\in\{1,...,N_{p}\}.

(4.14f)

Here,
\Delta_{h}:\mathcal{S}_{h}\to\left\{z_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}\mid\int_{\Omega}z_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=0\right\}
denotes the discrete Neumann–Laplacian such that
\Delta_{h}q_{h}
is the unique solution of

\displaystyle\left({\Delta_{h}q_{h},\zeta_{h}}\right)_{h}=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[(\Delta_{h}q_{h})\zeta_{h}\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla q_{h}\cdot\nabla\zeta_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=-\left({\nabla q_{h},\nabla\zeta_{h}}\right)_{L^{2}}\quad\quad\forall\ \zeta_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}.

(4.15)

We note for future reference, as
\{\mathcal{T}_{h}\}_{h>0}
is a quasi-uniform family of partitionings, and,
as the domain
\Omega
is convex, that

\displaystyle\left\lvert{q_{h}}\right\rvert_{W^{1,s}}\leq C\|{\Delta_{h}q_{h}}\|_{L^{2}}\qquad\forall\ q_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h},\ \forall\ s\in\left[1,\tfrac{2d}{d-2}\right),

(4.16)

see, e.g.,
[
8
, Lem. 3.1]
or
[
35
, Thm. 6.4]
.

Moreover, we define for all
t\in[t^{n-1},t^{n})
and

n\in\{1,...,N_{T}\}
the piecewise constant in time approximation of
\sigma_{\infty,h}
by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\infty,h}^{\Delta t,+}(t,\cdot)\coloneqq\sigma_{\infty,h}^{n}(\cdot)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\Delta t}\int_{t^{n-1}}^{t^{n}}\sigma_{\infty,h}(\tilde{t},\cdot)\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{t}\ \in\mathcal{S}_{h},

(4.17)

which fulfills

\displaystyle\Delta t\sum_{n=1}^{N_{T}}\|{\sigma_{\infty,h}^{n}}\|_{L^{2}({\partial\Omega})}^{2}=\|{\sigma_{\infty,h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}({\partial\Omega}))}^{2}\leq\left\|{\sigma_{\infty,h}}\right\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\partial\Omega))},

(4.18a)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\infty,h}^{\Delta t,+}\to\sigma_{\infty,h}\quad\text{strongly in }L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{S}_{h}),\quad\text{as }\Delta t\to 0.

(4.18b)

Furthermore, we make the following assumption on the discrete initial and boundary data which is needed for the limit process
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
.

Assumptions 4.3
.

Let
hold true. Then, in the limit
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
, we assume

\displaystyle\varphi_{h}^{0}

\displaystyle\to\varphi_{0}\quad

weakly

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{2}(\Omega),

(4.19a)

\displaystyle\sigma_{h}^{0}

\displaystyle\to\sigma_{0}\quad

weakly

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{2}(\Omega),

(4.19b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{0}

\displaystyle\to\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0}\quad

weakly

\displaystyle\text{ in }\mathbf{H},

(4.19c)

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{h}^{0}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{B}_{0}\quad

weakly

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}),

(4.19d)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\infty,h}|_{\partial\Omega}

\displaystyle\to\sigma_{\infty}|_{\partial\Omega}\quad

strongly

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\partial\Omega)).

(4.19e)

Remark 4.4
.

The assumptions (
4.14
) and (
4.19
) are no severe constraints in practice.
For example, let
hold true and
\psi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous. Then, the following choices for
\varphi_{h}^{0},\sigma_{h}^{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{0},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{0},\sigma_{\infty,h}
are in accordance with (
4.14
) and (
4.19
):

\displaystyle\varphi_{h}^{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{h}\varphi_{0},

(4.20a)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\sigma_{h}^{0}q_{h}\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\Delta t\int_{\Omega}\nabla\sigma_{h}^{0}\cdot\nabla q_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\Delta t\int_{\partial\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\sigma_{h}^{0}q_{h}\big]\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\sigma_{0}q_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\qquad

\displaystyle\forall\ q_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h},

(4.20b)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\Delta t\int_{\Omega}\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{0}:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\qquad

\displaystyle\forall\ \boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{h,\mathrm{div}},

(4.20c)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\mathbb{B}_{h}^{0}:\mathbb{C}_{h}\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\Delta t\int_{\Omega}\nabla\mathbb{B}_{h}^{0}:\nabla\mathbb{C}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{B}_{0}:\mathbb{C}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\qquad

\displaystyle\forall\ \mathbb{C}_{h}\in\mathcal{W}_{h},

(4.20d)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\infty,h}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{h}^{\mathrm{Cl}}\sigma_{\infty}.

(4.20e)

We note that (
4.14a
) follows from (
4.5
),
[
10
, eq. (3.16)]
and
. Moreover, (
4.14b
)–(
4.14d
) are a direct consequence of Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, (
4.9
), (
4.10
) and
.
As we have a triangulation with non-obtuse simplices, (
4.14f
) follows from
and
[
6
, Lem. 5.2]
.
Furthermore, (
4.13c
) and
yield (
4.14e
).

Moreover,
and the error estimates (
4.5
) and (
4.13c
) imply (
4.19a
) and (
4.19e
), respectively.
Besides, (
4.19b
) follows from
, (
4.14b
), (
4.11
), (
4.12
), the denseness of
H^{1}(\Omega)
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
and the fact that for all
q\in H^{1}(\Omega)
there exists a sequence
\{q_{h}\}_{h>0}\subset\mathcal{S}_{h}
such that
\|{q_{h}-q}\|_{H^{1}}\to 0
, as
h\to 0
.
Similarly, (
4.19c
) follows from
, (
4.14c
), the denseness of
\mathbf{V}
in
\mathbf{H}
and the fact that for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}\in\mathbf{V}
there exists a sequence
\{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\}_{h>0}\subset\mathcal{V}_{h,\mathrm{div}}
such that
\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}}\|_{H^{1}}\to 0
, as
h\to 0
, which is due to (
4.2
). The remaining identity (
4.19d
) follows with similar arguments.

4.2
A regularized fully discrete finite element approximation

Now, for given
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
, we introduce a fully discrete approximation of
.
There are several difficulties on the fully discrete level which have to be taken into account.
One of the most important issues arises
from the fact that
\mathbb{B}_{h}\in\mathcal{W}_{h}
only implies
\mathcal{I}_{h}[G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}_{h})]\in\mathcal{W}_{h}
, as in general
G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}_{h})\not\in\mathcal{W}_{h}
. For that reason, it is not clear that the analogues of (
3.33
)–(
3.35
) can be performed on the discrete level, especially controlling the convective term in (
3.31
).

Here, the approach of Barrett and Boyaval
[
6
, Sec. 5]
is very helpful.
We recall the fourth order tensorial function
\Lambda_{\delta}:\mathcal{W}_{h}\to\mathbb{R}^{d^{4}}
, where the symmetric
(d\times d)
-matrix
\Lambda_{\delta,i,j}(\mathbb{B}_{h})
approximates
\delta_{i,j}\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h})
in a certain sense, where
i,j\in\{1,...,d\}
and
\mathbb{B}_{h}\in\mathcal{W}_{h}
and
\delta_{i,j}
denotes the Kronecker delta. The reason for introducing this nonlinear quantity is to control the discrete version of the convective term from (
3.31
), which is due to the following property:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d}\Lambda_{\delta,i,j}(\mathbb{B}_{h}):\partial_{x_{j}}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}_{h})\big]=\partial_{x_{i}}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\trace\big(H_{\delta}(G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}_{h}))\big)\big]\quad\text{ on }K_{k},

(4.21)

for
k\in\{1,...,N_{k}\}
and
i\in\{1,...,d\}
, see
[
6
, eq. (5.17)]
,
which will make it possible to derive an
a priori
estimate on the fully discrete level.
As the family of partitionings
\{\mathcal{T}_{h}\}_{h>0}
is quasi-uniform, it follows from the definition of
\Lambda_{\delta,i,j}
(cf.
[
6
, Sec. 5.1]
) that

\displaystyle\|{\Lambda_{\delta,i,j}(\mathbb{B}_{h})}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\|{\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h})}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\quad\forall\ \mathbb{B}_{h}\in\mathcal{W}_{h}.

(4.22)

Next, we present an approximation of
for which we explain the motivation afterwards.

Problem
:

Let
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
. For given discrete initial and boundary data satisfying (
4.14
) and
n\in\{1,...,N_{T}\}
, find the discrete solution
(\varphi_{h}^{n},\mu_{h}^{n},\sigma_{h}^{n},p_{h}^{n},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\in(\mathcal{S}_{h})^{4}\times\mathcal{V}_{h}\times\mathcal{W}_{h}
which satisfies, for any
(\zeta_{h},\varrho_{h},\xi_{h},q_{h},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h},\mathbb{C}_{h})\in(\mathcal{S}_{h})^{4}\times\mathcal{V}_{h}\times\mathcal{W}_{h}
:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\Big(\frac{\varphi_{h}^{n}-\varphi_{h}^{n-1}}{\Delta t}-\Gamma_{\varphi,h}^{n}\Big)\zeta_{h}\Big]+\mathcal{I}_{h}[m(\varphi_{h}^{n-1})]\nabla\mu_{h}^{n}\cdot\nabla\zeta_{h}-\varphi_{h}^{n-1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}\cdot\nabla\zeta_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(4.23a)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\Big(-\mu_{h}^{n}+A\psi_{1}^{\prime}(\varphi_{h}^{n})+A\psi_{2}^{\prime}(\varphi_{h}^{n-1})-\chi_{\varphi}\sigma_{h}^{n}\Big)\varrho_{h}\Big]+B\nabla\varphi_{h}^{n}\cdot\nabla\varrho_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(4.23b)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\Big(\frac{\sigma_{h}^{n}-\sigma_{h}^{n-1}}{\Delta t}+\Gamma_{\sigma,h}^{n}\Big)\xi_{h}\Big]+\mathcal{I}_{h}[n(\varphi_{h}^{n-1})]\nabla(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma_{h}^{n}-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi_{h}^{n})\cdot\nabla\xi_{h}-\sigma_{h}^{n-1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}\cdot\nabla\xi_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\partial\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[K\big(\sigma_{h}^{n}-\sigma_{\infty,h}^{n}\big)\xi_{h}\Big]\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1},

(4.23c)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}}\right)q_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(4.23d)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}}{\Delta t}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}\cdot\nabla\right)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}\cdot\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}\cdot\nabla\right)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\right)+2\mathcal{I}_{h}[\eta(\varphi_{h}^{n-1})]\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}):\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h})\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Omega}\kappa\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})-\mathbb{I}\big]:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}-\operatorname{div}\left({\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}}\right)p_{h}^{n}+\big(\varphi_{h}^{n-1}\nabla\mu_{h}^{n}+\sigma_{h}^{n-1}\nabla(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma_{h}^{n}-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi_{h}^{n})\big)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(4.23e)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\Big(\frac{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n-1}}{\Delta t}+\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{n-1})}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-\mathbb{I})\Big):\mathbb{C}_{h}\Big]-2\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}^{n}_{h}:\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\mathbb{C}_{h}\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big]+\alpha\nabla\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}:\nabla\mathbb{C}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{\Omega}\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{d}[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}]_{i}\Lambda_{\delta,i,j}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}):\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbb{C}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(4.23f)

where we define

\Gamma_{\varphi,h}^{n}\coloneqq\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi_{h}^{n},\sigma_{h}^{n},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})
and
\Gamma_{\sigma,h}^{n}\coloneqq\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi_{h}^{n},\sigma_{h}^{n})
.

Let us now motivate the idea for
by explaining the derivation from the weak formulation of
in the sense of Definition
3.2
.
First, the
\delta
-regularization strategy from Section
3.4
is applied and, as we use finite element functions for the approximation in space, we also use the fourth order tensor
\Lambda_{\delta}
to control the convection term for
\mathbb{B}
on the discrete level in (
4.23
). Besides, a semi-implicit time discretization of first order is chosen where linear terms are treated fully implicitly and most of the nonlinear terms are treated explicitly. In (
4.23b
), a convex-concave splitting for the potential
\psi=\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}
is chosen, which allows the inequality

\displaystyle\left(\psi_{1}^{\prime}(\varphi_{h}^{n})+\psi_{2}^{\prime}(\varphi_{h}^{n-1})\right)(\varphi_{h}^{n}-\varphi_{h}^{n-1})\geq\psi(\varphi_{h}^{n})-\psi(\varphi_{h}^{n-1}).

(4.24)

Besides, the nonlinear source terms
\Gamma_{\varphi},\Gamma_{\sigma}
are treated fully implicitly and the nonlinear functions
n,m,\eta,\tau
are treated explicitly, but also a different time approximation can be chosen for these terms.
The remaining terms are approximated in a way such that stability of the scheme
can be shown, see Lemma
4.7
.
Furthermore, we make use of numerical integration in terms of the nodal interpolation operator
\mathcal{I}_{h}
. On the one hand, this can reduce the computational effort as the mass matrices are diagonal, whereas on the other hand, the nodal interpolation operator in (
4.23
) and in the second line in (
4.23
) is required for stability of the scheme.

Remark 4.5
.

In the literature, the velocity field in Navier–Stokes systems is sometimes approximated with finite element functions where the constraint (
4.23d
) is directly included in the finite element space
\mathcal{V}_{h,\mathrm{div}}
. Hence, the velocity field and the test functions in equation (
4.23
) would belong to the finite element space
\mathcal{V}_{h,\mathrm{div}}
, and (
4.23
) would be replaced by

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}}{\Delta t}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}\cdot\nabla\right)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}\cdot\left(\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}\cdot\nabla\right)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\right)+2\mathcal{I}_{h}[\eta(\varphi_{h}^{n-1})]\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}):\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h})\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Omega}\kappa\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})-\mathbb{I}\big]:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}+\big(\varphi_{h}^{n-1}\nabla\mu_{h}^{n}+\sigma_{h}^{n-1}\nabla(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma_{h}^{n}-\chi_{\varphi}\varphi_{h}^{n})\big)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(4.25)

for all
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{h,\mathrm{div}}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{h,\mathrm{div}}
are the solution from the previous time step and the unknown solution from the current time step, respectively.
The unknown pressure
p_{h}^{n}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
, which is unique up to an additive constant, can be reconstructed afterwards as the discrete LBB stability condition (
4.2
) is fulfilled, see, e.g.,
[
34
, Chap. I, Lem. 4.1]
or
[
13
, Lem. 4.2]
.
However, it is rather hard to construct test functions
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\in\mathcal{V}_{h,\mathrm{div}}
in practice.
This is the reason why we use (
4.23
) instead of (
4.5
).

4.3
Stability of the regularized discrete system

We now introduce the discrete energy
\mathcal{F}_{\delta,h}:\mathcal{S}_{h}\times\mathcal{S}_{h}\times\mathcal{V}_{h}\times\mathcal{W}_{h}\to\mathbb{R}
of the problem
given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{\delta,h}(\varphi_{h},\sigma_{h},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h},\mathbb{B}_{h})&=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[A\psi(\varphi_{h})+\frac{\chi_{\sigma}}{2}\left\lvert{\sigma_{h}}\right\rvert^{2}+\chi_{\varphi}\sigma_{h}(1-\varphi_{h})+\frac{\kappa}{2}\trace\big(\mathbb{B}_{h}-G_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h})\big)\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega}\frac{B}{2}\left\lvert{\nabla\varphi_{h}}\right\rvert^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,\end{split}

(4.26)

for all
(\varphi_{h},\sigma_{h},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h},\mathbb{B}_{h})\in\mathcal{S}_{h}\times\mathcal{S}_{h}\times\mathcal{V}_{h}\times\mathcal{W}_{h}
, where
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
.
We remark that it is not guaranteed that
\mathcal{F}_{\delta,h}
is non-negative as the term
\sigma_{h}(1-\varphi_{h})
can have a negative sign. This is one of the main difficulties we have to handle in the derivation of useful
a priori
estimates.

For future reference, we note the elementary identity

\displaystyle 2x(x-y)=x^{2}-y^{2}+(x-y)^{2}\quad\quad\forall\ x,y\in\mathbb{R}.

(4.27)

Moreover, we recall the following discrete version of Gronwall’s inequality, i.e. Lemma
3.5
. For a proof, we refer to, e.g.,
[
20
, pp. 401–402]
.

Lemma 4.6
.

Assume that
e_{n},a_{n},b_{n}\geq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Then

\displaystyle e_{n}\leq a_{n}+\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}b_{i}e_{i}\quad\forall\ n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad e_{n}\leq a_{n}\cdot\exp\Big(\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}b_{i}\Big)\quad\forall\ n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(4.28)

With the help of Lemma
4.6
, we derive stability bounds for solutions of
.

Lemma 4.7

(Stability)
.

Let
–
hold true and let
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Suppose that the discrete initial and boundary data satisfy (
4.14
) and assume that
\Delta t<\Delta t_{*}
, where the constant
\Delta t_{*}
depends only on the model parameters and is defined in (
4.43
). Then, for
n\in\{1,...,N_{T}\}
, a solution
(\varphi_{h}^{n}
,
\mu_{h}^{n}
,
\sigma_{h}^{n}
,
p_{h}^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}
,
\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\in(\mathcal{S}_{h})^{4}\times\mathcal{V}_{h}\times\mathcal{W}_{h}
to the problem
, if it exists, satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\max_{n=1,...,N_{T}}\Big(\|{\varphi_{h}^{n}}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|{\sigma_{h}^{n}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\left\lvert{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}}\right\rvert\big]}\right\|_{L^{1}}+\frac{1}{\delta}\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\left\lvert{[\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}]_{-}}\right\rvert\big]}\right\|_{L^{1}}\Big)\\
&\quad+\sum_{n=1}^{N_{T}}\Big(\|{\nabla\varphi_{h}^{n}-\nabla\varphi_{h}^{n-1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\sigma_{h}^{n}-\sigma_{h}^{n-1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)\\
&\quad+\Delta t\sum_{n=1}^{N_{T}}\Big(\|{\mu_{h}^{n}}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|{\nabla\sigma_{h}^{n}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\sigma_{h}^{n}}\|_{L^{2}({\partial\Omega})}^{2}+\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)\\
&\quad+\Delta t\sum_{n=1}^{N_{T}}\bigg(\alpha\delta^{2}\left\|{\nabla\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big]}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\trace\big(\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})+[\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})]^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x\bigg)\\
&\leq C(T)\Big(1+\left\lvert{\mathcal{F}_{h,\delta}(\varphi_{h}^{0},\sigma_{h}^{0},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{0},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{0})}\right\rvert+\Delta t\sum_{n=1}^{N_{T}}\|{\sigma_{\infty,h}^{n}}\|_{L^{2}(\partial\Omega)}^{2}\Big)\leq C(T),\end{split}

(4.29)

where the constants
C(T)
are independent of
h,\Delta t,\alpha,\delta
, but depend exponentially on
T
.

We now start with the testing procedure.
First, we choose
\zeta_{h}=\mu_{h}^{n}
in (
4.23a
),
\varrho_{h}=\frac{1}{\Delta t}(\varphi_{h}^{n}-\varphi_{h}^{n-1})
in (
4.23b
),
\xi_{h}=\chi_{\sigma}\sigma_{h}^{n}+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi_{h}^{n})
in (
4.23
) and
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}
in (
4.23
) and sum up the resulting equations. Then, we obtain on noting
,
, (
4.24
) and (
4.27
), that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{B}{2\Delta t}\Big(\|{\nabla\varphi_{h}^{n}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|{\nabla\varphi_{h}^{n-1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\nabla\varphi_{h}^{n}-\nabla\varphi_{h}^{n-1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)+\int_{\Omega}\frac{A}{\Delta t}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\psi(\varphi_{h}^{n})-\psi(\varphi_{h}^{n-1})\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\quad+\frac{\chi_{\sigma}}{2\Delta t}\Big(\|{\sigma_{h}^{n}}\|_{h}^{2}-\|{\sigma_{h}^{n-1}}\|_{h}^{2}+\|{\sigma_{h}^{n}-\sigma_{h}^{n-1}}\|_{h}^{2}\Big)+\frac{1}{2\Delta t}\Big(\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\Big)\\
&\quad+m_{0}\|{\nabla\mu_{h}^{n}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+n_{0}\left\|{\chi_{\sigma}\nabla\sigma_{h}^{n}-\chi_{\varphi}\nabla\varphi_{h}^{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+K\chi_{\sigma}\|{\sigma_{h}^{n}}\|_{h,{\partial\Omega}}^{2}+2\eta_{0}\|{\mathbb{D}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n})}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\Gamma_{\sigma,h}^{n}\big(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma_{h}^{n}+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi_{h}^{n})\big)-\mu_{h}^{n}\Gamma_{\varphi,h}^{n}+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi_{h}^{n})\Big(\frac{\sigma_{h}^{n}-\sigma_{h}^{n-1}}{\Delta t}\Big)-\frac{\varphi_{h}^{n}-\varphi_{h}^{n-1}}{\Delta t}\chi_{\varphi}\sigma_{h}^{n}\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x\\
&\quad+\int_{\Omega}\kappa\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})-\mathbb{I}\big]:\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[K\chi_{\varphi}\sigma_{h}^{n}(1-\varphi_{h}^{n})-K\sigma_{\infty,h}^{n}\big(\chi_{\sigma}\sigma_{h}^{n}+\chi_{\varphi}(1-\varphi_{h}^{n})\big)\Big]\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\\
&\leq 0.\end{split}

(4.30)

Next, we test (
4.23
) with
\mathbb{C}_{h}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\big(\mathbb{I}-\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big]\big)
. This gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\Big(\frac{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n-1}}{\Delta t}+\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{n-1})}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-\mathbb{I})\Big):\frac{\kappa}{2}\big(\mathbb{I}-G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big)\Big]-2\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}^{n}_{h}:\mathcal{I}_{h}\left[\frac{\kappa}{2}\big(\mathbb{I}-G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big)\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega}\frac{\alpha\kappa}{2}\nabla\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}:\nabla\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big]+\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{d}[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}]_{i}\Lambda_{\delta,i,j}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}):\frac{\kappa}{2}\partial_{x_{j}}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

We now estimate the terms on the right-hand side.
Together with (
3.29e
), it follows that

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n-1}}{\Delta t}\Big):\big(\mathbb{I}-G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big)\geq\frac{\trace\big(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-G_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big)-\trace\big(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n-1}-G_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n-1})\big)}{\Delta t}.

We have on noting (
3.29a
), (
3.29b
) and (
3.29d
) that

\displaystyle(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-\mathbb{I}):\big(\mathbb{I}-G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big)\geq\big(\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})-\mathbb{I}\big):\big(\mathbb{I}-G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big)=\trace\big(\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})+[\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})]^{-1}-2\mathbb{I}\big)\geq 0.

Moreover, using (
3.29a
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}2\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}^{n}_{h}:\mathcal{I}_{h}\left[\frac{\kappa}{2}\big(\mathbb{I}-G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big)\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\right]\,{\mathrm{d}}x=\int_{\Omega}\kappa(\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}):\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\beta_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})-\mathbb{I}\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

On noting
[
6
, Lem. 5.1]
we obtain

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}\frac{\alpha\kappa}{2}\nabla\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}:\nabla\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x\geq\int_{\Omega}\frac{\alpha\kappa\delta^{2}}{2}\left\lvert{\nabla\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big]}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

As
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}\in\mathcal{V}_{h,\text{div}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\trace\big(H_{\delta}(G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}))\big)\Big]\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
, we get with (
4.21
) and integration by parts that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{d}[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}]_{i}\Lambda_{\delta,i,j}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}):\frac{\kappa}{2}\partial_{x_{j}}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[G^{\prime}_{\delta}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}\cdot\nabla\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\trace\big(H_{\delta}(G_{\delta}^{\prime}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}))\big)\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x=0.

Therefore, on noting
, we have the inequality
