\displaystyle\|{\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1})}+\|{\Delta_{h}\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}+\|{\partial_{t}\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;(H^{1})^{\prime})}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta t}}\|{\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t}-\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,\pm}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}

\displaystyle\quad+\|{\mu_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}+\|{\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2})}+\|{\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}+\|{\partial_{t}\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t}}\|_{L^{4/d}(0,T;(H^{1})^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta t}}\|{\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t}-\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,\pm}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}+\|{\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}({\partial\Omega}))}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2})}+\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}

\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta t}}\|{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{\Delta t}-\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{\Delta t,\pm}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq C(T),

(4.76a)

and, for any
l\in\{1,...,N_{T}\}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-l\Delta t}\left\|{\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}(\cdot,t+l\Delta t)-\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}(\cdot,t)}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\leq C(T)l\Delta t.

(4.76b)

Further, if in addition
and the CFL constraint (
4.58
) hold true, then there exist constants
C(T,\alpha^{-1})>0
depending on
T,\alpha^{-1}
but not on
h,\Delta t
, such that

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{S}\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{\Delta t}}\|_{L^{4/3}(0,T;H^{1})}+\|{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2})}+\|{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}+\|{\partial_{t}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t}}\|_{L^{4/3}(0,T;(H^{1})^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta t}}\|{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t}-\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,\pm}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\leq C(T,\alpha^{-1}),

(4.76c)

and, for any
l\in\{1,...,N_{T}\}
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T-l\Delta t}\left\|{\mathcal{R}_{h}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}(\cdot,t+l\Delta t)-\mathcal{R}_{h}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{\Delta t(,\pm)}(\cdot,t)}\right\|_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\leq C(T,\alpha^{-1})\Big(l^{\frac{3}{4}}\Delta t+h^{2}\Big).

(4.76d)

For the main result, we recall the following technical result taken from
[
6
, Lem. 5.3]
.

Lemma 4.16
.

For all
K_{k}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, and for all
\mathbb{C}_{h}\in\mathcal{W}_{h,\mathrm{PD}}
, it holds

\displaystyle\max\limits_{i,j=1,...,d}\int_{K_{k}}\left\lvert{\Lambda_{i,j}(\mathbb{C}_{h})-\mathbb{C}_{h}\delta_{ij}}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\leq Ch^{2}\int_{K_{k}}\left\lvert{\nabla\mathbb{C}_{h}}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

(4.86)

We also recall the following result from
[
7
, Lem. 6.8]
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
g\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R})
with Lipschitz constant
L_{g}
. For all
K_{k}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
and for all
q_{h}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
,
\mathbb{C}_{h}\in\mathcal{W}_{h}
, it holds

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{K_{k}}\left\lvert{\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[g(q_{h})\big]-g(q_{h})}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x&\leq CL_{g}^{2}h^{2}\int_{K_{k}}\left\lvert{\nabla q_{h}}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,\\
\int_{K_{k}}\left\lvert{\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[g(\mathbb{C}_{h})\big]-g(\mathbb{C}_{h})}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x&\leq CL_{g}^{2}h^{2}\int_{K_{k}}\left\lvert{\nabla\mathbb{C}_{h}}\right\rvert^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x.\end{split}

(4.87)

We now pass to the limit
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
in
and show that the function tuple

from Lemma

in the sense of Definition

.
For the reader’s convenience, we state this result in the following theorem.

Note that in comparison to Lemma
4.15
we need the additional assumption
on the source terms
\Gamma_{\varphi},\Gamma_{\sigma}
, as otherwise the limit passing would not be possible in presence of
\mathcal{I}_{h}
. However, this assumption is not strict in practice and can be dropped if, e.g., mass lumping is not considered.

Theorem 4.18

(Limit passing in
)
.

Let
–
hold true. Suppose that the discrete initial and boundary data satisfy (
4.14
) and (
4.19
), and that the CFL constraint (
4.58
) holds.
Then the functions
\varphi,\mu,\sigma,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}},\mathbb{B}
from Lemma
4.15
form a weak solution to
in the sense of Definition
3.2
.

Let
\zeta_{h}=\mathcal{I}_{h}\zeta
, where
\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}\big(0,T;C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})\big)
. The limit passing in the linear terms in (
4.75a
) can be established with a straightforward calculation from
[

, Thm. 6.3]

4.11
), (
4.5
), (
4.76

We now pass to the limit in the nonlinear terms in (

).
On noting the continuity of
m(\cdot)
, (
()
‣
4.15
), (
4.5
), (
4.7
) and applying the generalised Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem
[

, Chap. 3]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[m(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,-})\big]\nabla\zeta_{h}-m(\varphi)\nabla\zeta}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\to 0,

as
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
. Then, together with (
4.78
), we obtain by the product of weak-strong convergence
[

, Chap. 8]

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[m(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,-})\big]\nabla\zeta_{h}\cdot\nabla\mu_{h}^{\Delta t,+}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t\to\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}m(\varphi)\nabla\zeta\cdot\nabla\mu\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t,

as
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
.

In order to pass to the limit in the source term in (
4.75a
), we proceed as follows:

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\left({\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}),\zeta_{h}}\right)_{h}-\left({\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}),\zeta}\right)_{L^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\left({\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+})\big],\zeta_{h}}\right)_{h}-\left({\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+})\big],\zeta_{h}}\right)_{L^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\quad+\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\left({\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+})\big]-\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}),\zeta_{h}}\right)_{L^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\quad+\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\left({\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+})-\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}),\zeta_{h}}\right)_{L^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\left({\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B}),\zeta_{h}-\zeta}\right)_{L^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\eqqcolon Ia+Ib+Ic+Id.

On noting Hölder’s inequality, (
4.11
), (
4.6
), (
4.5
), (
4.9
) and
, it holds

\displaystyle\begin{split}Ia&\leq Ch\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+})\big]}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\|{\zeta_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}\\
&\leq Ch\Big(1+\|{\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}+\|{\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\Big)\|{\zeta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2})}.\end{split}

(4.88)

Let us remark that an analogue of (

instead of

.
Then, we receive on noting Hölder’s inequality, (

\displaystyle\begin{split}Ib&\leq C\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+})\big]-\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+},\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+})}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\|{\zeta_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\\
&\leq Ch\left(\|{\nabla\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}+\|{\nabla\sigma_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}+\|{\nabla\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}\right)\|{\zeta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2})}.\end{split}

(4.89)

Hence, on noting (

‣

-term in (
4.75a
), and the
\Gamma_{\sigma}
-term analogously to the
\Gamma_{\varphi}
-term, respectively. For the term containing
\Lambda_{i,j}(\cdot)
, the limit passing is based on (
4.76
), (
4.5
), (
()
‣
4.15
), (
()
‣
4.15
) and the error estimate (
4.86
).

For the reader’s convenience, we show the passage to the limit in the second and the third term of (
4.75
). Let us define
\mathbb{C}_{h}=\mathcal{I}_{h}\mathbb{C}
, where
\mathbb{C}\in C_{0}^{\infty}\big(0,T;C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\text{S}})\big)
.
It holds

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\bigg[\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,-})}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}:\mathbb{C}_{h}\bigg]-\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{B}:\mathbb{C}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\big(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I}\big)\bigg[\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}:\mathcal{I}_{h}\bigg[\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,-})}\mathbb{C}_{h}\bigg]\bigg]\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\quad+\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}:\mathcal{I}_{h}\bigg[\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,-})}\mathbb{C}_{h}\bigg]-\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{B}:\mathbb{C}\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert\eqqcolon IIa+IIb,

where
\mathcal{I}
denotes the identity.
For the first term, it holds with (
4.11
), (
4.6
),
, Hölder’s inequality, (
4.8
) and (
4.9
) that

\displaystyle IIa

\displaystyle\leq Ch\int_{0}^{T}\|{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{H^{1}}\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\bigg[\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,-})}\mathbb{C}_{h}\bigg]}\right\|_{L^{2}}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\leq Ch\|{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}\|{\mathbb{C}_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})},

which yields, on noting (
4.76
) and (
4.5
), that
IIa
vanishes as
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
.
Moreover, it holds by the continuity of
\tau(\cdot)
, (
()
‣
4.15
), (
4.7
), a triangle inequality and as
\mathcal{I}_{h}\mathbb{C}_{h}=\mathcal{I}_{h}\mathbb{C}
, that

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\left[\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,-})}\mathbb{C}_{h}\right]-\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{C}}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}=\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\left[\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,-})}\mathbb{C}\right]-\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{C}}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,-})}-\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\right)\mathbb{C}\right]}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}+\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\left[\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{C}\right]-\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{C}}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\left\|{\left(\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{\Delta t,-})}-\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\right)\mathbb{C}}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{\infty})}+C\left\|{\mathcal{I}_{h}\left[\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{C}\right]-\frac{1}{\tau(\varphi)}\mathbb{C}}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{\infty})}

\displaystyle\quad\to 0,

as
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
.
We finally obtain
IIb\to 0
, as
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
, by using (
()
‣
4.15
) and the product of weak-strong convergence
[
3
, Chap. 8]
.

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}:\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\mathbb{C}_{h}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}\big]-\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}:(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{B})\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}:(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I})\big[\mathbb{C}_{h}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}:(\mathbb{C}_{h}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+})-\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}:(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{B})\,{\mathrm{d}}x\,{\mathrm{d}}t}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\eqqcolon IIIa+IIIb.

Similarly to (
4.87
), it holds for all
k\in\{1,...,N_{k}\}
and
K_{k}\in\mathcal{T}_{h}
, that

\displaystyle\left\|{(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I})\big[\mathbb{C}_{h}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}\big]}\right\|_{L^{2}(K_{k})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ch^{2}\Big(\|{\mathbb{C}_{h}}\|_{L^{\infty}(K_{k})}^{2}\|{\nabla\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(K_{k})}^{2}+\|{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{\infty}(K_{k})}^{2}\|{\nabla\mathbb{C}_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(K_{k})}^{2}\Big).

(4.90)

It follows with (
4.6
) and the Sobolev embedding
H^{1}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{s}(\Omega)
, where
s\in(2,\infty)
for
d=2
, that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\|{(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I})\big[\mathbb{C}_{h}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}\big]}\right\|_{L^{2}(K_{k})}^{2}&\leq Ch^{2-\frac{4}{s}}\Big(\|{\mathbb{C}_{h}}\|_{L^{s}(K_{k})}^{2}\|{\nabla\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(K_{k})}^{2}+\|{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{s}(K_{k})}^{2}\|{\nabla\mathbb{C}_{h}}\|_{L^{2}(K_{k})}^{2}\Big)\\
&\leq Ch^{2-\frac{4}{s}}\|{\mathbb{C}_{h}}\|_{H^{1}(K_{k})}^{2}\|{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{H^{1}(K_{k})}^{2}.\end{split}

(4.91)

Summing over all
k=1,...,N_{k}
and integrating with respect to the time variable yield

\displaystyle\left\|{(\mathcal{I}_{h}-\mathcal{I})\big[\mathbb{C}_{h}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}\big]}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2})}^{2}\leq Ch^{2-\frac{4}{s}}\|{\mathbb{C}_{h}}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;H^{1})}^{2}\|{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1})}^{2}.

(4.92)

Then, with Hölder’s inequality, (
4.92
), (
4.76
) and (
4.5
), we obtain that
IIIa\to 0
, as
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
.
Furthermore, on noting (
4.5
) and (
()
‣
4.15
) we obtain that
\mathbb{C}_{h}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{\Delta t,+}\to\mathbb{C}\mathbb{B}
strongly in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}))
, as
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
. Hence, by the product of weak-strong convergence
[
3
, Chap. 8]
and (
()
‣
4.15
), we receive that
IIIb\to 0
, as
(h,\Delta t)\to(0,0)
.

By the denseness of
C_{0}^{\infty}(0,T;C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\text{S}}))
in
L^{4}(0,T;H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}_{\text{S}}))
, we finally obtain that (
3.10e
) is fulfilled in the stated sense. This proves the theorem.
∎

5
Numerical results

In this section, we present numerical results for the scheme
that was analyzed in Section
4
.

5.1
Computational aspects

5.1.1
Description of the solution algorithm

Before presenting the numerical results, we first discuss the solution strategy.
On one side, one could think of applying Newton’s method to solve the nonlinear system of equations
as it can provide good error reduction rates.
However, Newton’s method would be too expensive and would require too much memory, as the coupled system of equations
is very large, and is hence not useful in practice.
On the other side, making use of a fixed point iteration allows to decouple the system of equation
into linear subsystems (
4.48a
)–(
4.48b
), (
4.48
), (
4.48d
)–(
4.48
) and (
4.48
) that can be solved separately. But here, a very small time step size
\Delta t
is required such that the fixed point iteration can converge. Therefore, a fixed point iteration would need too much computing time in practice.
Moreover, numerical experiments indicate that the subsystem (
4.48a
)–(
4.48b
) requires additional consideration and the most precision which is because of the scaling with
B=\beta\epsilon
and
A=\frac{\beta}{\epsilon}
with
\epsilon>0
very small.

For these reasons, we propose an inner-outer type algorithm to solve the nonlinear coupled scheme
.
For the outer iteration, we apply a fixed point-like strategy, where
is decoupled into the subsystems (
4.48a
)–(
4.48b
), (
4.48
), (
4.48d
)–(
4.48
) and (
4.48
), where all nonlinear terms are treated explicitly except of
A\psi_{1}^{\prime}(\varphi_{h}^{n})
in (
4.48b
) which is treated implicitly.
Hence, we first solve the nonlinear subsystem (
4.48a
)–(
4.48b
) with Newton’s method, where the resulting linear systems are solved with a preconditioned BICGSTAB-method. After that, we solve the linear subsystems (
4.48
), (
4.48d
)–(
4.48
) and (
4.48
) separately with an AMG-preconditioned MINRES-solver, an AMG-preconditioned GMRES-method and an AMG-preconditioned MINRES-solver, respectively. The algorithm is implemented with the finite element toolbox FEniCS
[
45
]
which also provides the iterative linear solvers and the preconditioners.

However, due to limited computational possibilities with the finite element toolbox FEniCS, we consider (
4.48
) with
\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}[\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}]_{i}\Lambda_{i,j}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}):\partial_{x_{j}}\mathbb{C}_{h}
replaced by
\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}:((\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{C}_{h})
, which however is a good approximation due to (
4.86
), and, we replace
\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}:\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\mathbb{C}_{h}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}\big]
by
\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}:(\mathbb{C}_{h}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})
.
To increase the accuracy of our numerical solutions, we make use of a mesh refinement strategy, similarly to
[
32
]
, where the mesh is locally refined near the interface where
\left\lvert{\varphi_{h}^{n-1}}\right\rvert\leq 1-\delta
, for a small
\delta>0
, where the local mesh size corresponds to a uniform
N_{f}\times N_{f}
grid. Away from the interface, where
\left\lvert{\varphi_{h}^{n-1}}\right\rvert>1-\delta
, a coarse mesh is used with a local mesh size corresponding to a uniform
N_{c}\times N_{c}
grid.

5.1.2
Specification of the parameters, model functions and initial data

Now we specify the parameters, model functions and initial data, where our choices are motivated by, e.g.,
[
23
,
31
]
.
We perform the calculations on the domain
\Omega=(-5,5)^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and we use the model functions

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varphi}(\varphi,\sigma,\mathbb{B})=h(1.1\varphi)\big(\mathcal{P}\sigma f(\mathbb{B})-\mathcal{A}\big),\qquad

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi,\sigma)=\mathcal{C}h(\varphi)\sigma,\qquad

\displaystyle m(\varphi)=2\left(h(\varphi)\right)^{2}+m_{0},

\displaystyle\eta(\varphi)=\eta_{-1}h(-\varphi)+\eta_{1}h(\varphi),\qquad

\displaystyle\tau(\varphi)=\tau_{-1}h(-\varphi)+\tau_{1}h(\varphi),\qquad

\displaystyle n(\varphi)=n_{0},

where the interpolation function with cut-offs
h(\cdot)
is defined in (
3.6a
) and
f(\cdot)
is defined in (
3.6c
).
Unless otherwise stated, we choose the parameters

\displaystyle

\displaystyle N_{c}=32,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle N_{f}=1024,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\delta=0.075,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\Delta t=5\cdot 10^{-4},\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\epsilon=0.01,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\beta=0.1,\hskip 9.24994pt

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}=2,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=0,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}=10,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\chi_{\sigma}=500,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\chi_{\varphi}=10,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\alpha=10^{-3},\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle m_{0}=10^{-12},\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle n_{0}=0.002,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle K=1000,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\eta_{1}=5000,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\eta_{-1}=5000,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\kappa=10^{4},\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}}{\kappa}=1,\hskip 9.24994pt

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{-1}}{\kappa}=1.

Therefore,
h_{\max}=10\cdot 2^{-5}=0.3125
is the maximal diameter and
h_{\min}=10\cdot 2^{-10}\approx 0.009766
is the minimal diameter of all triangular elements.
Moreover, note that the assumptions
–
are fulfilled with these choices.
Actually, the (modified) potential with quadratic growth from (
3.7
) should be used such that the growth assumptions in
are fulfilled.
However, for simplicity, we use the (unmodified) potential

\displaystyle\psi(\varphi)=\tfrac{1}{4}(1-\varphi^{2})^{2},\quad\text{where}\quad\psi^{\prime}(\varphi)=\psi_{1}^{\prime}(\varphi)+\psi_{2}^{\prime}(\varphi)=\varphi^{3}-\varphi,

as the order parameter
\varphi
always stays very close to the interval
[-1,1]
in our numerical experiments. Also note the scaling with
1.1\varphi
in the source term
\Gamma_{\varphi}
, as
\varphi\approx{-1}
in practice in the pure healthy phase and we want to exclude proliferation effects there.

For the initial tumour profile, we set
\varphi_{h}^{0}=\mathcal{I}_{h}\varphi_{0}
, where
\varphi_{0}\in C(\overline{\Omega})
is a slightly perturbed sphere given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=-\tanh\Big(\frac{r(x)}{\sqrt{2}\epsilon}\Big),\quad\text{where}\quad r(x)=\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert-\frac{5}{12}(2+0.2\cos(2\theta)),

(5.2)

where
x=\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta))^{T}\in\Omega
.
We choose
\sigma_{h}^{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
as the solution of the quasi-static equation

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}n_{0}\big(\chi_{\sigma}\nabla\sigma_{h}^{0}-\chi_{\varphi}\nabla\varphi_{h}^{0}\big)\cdot\nabla\xi_{h}+\mathcal{I}_{h}\big[\Gamma_{\sigma}(\varphi_{h}^{0},\sigma_{h}^{0})\xi_{h}\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}x+\int_{\partial\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[K(\sigma_{h}^{0}-\sigma_{\infty})\xi_{h}\Big]\ \mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}=0,

(5.3)

for all
\xi\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
, where
\sigma_{\infty}\coloneqq 1
in the numerical experiments unless otherwise stated.
Moreover, we assume no initial velocity and no initial elastic stresses.
More precisely, we start with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{0}^{h}(x)=(0,0)^{T},\qquad\mathbb{B}_{0}^{h}(x)

\displaystyle=\text{diag}(1,1),

(5.4)

where
x\in\Omega
. The initial profile
\varphi_{h}^{0}
, the initial nutrient
\sigma_{h}^{0}
and the initial mesh are shown in Figure
4
.
It is easy to verify that the initial and boundary values satisfy the assumptions (
4.14
).

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 4:
Initial tumour (left), initial nutrient (center) and initial mesh (right).

In the following, we will systematically interpret the influence of different parameters in our model.
The influence of the parameters
\epsilon,\beta,\mathcal{P},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C},\chi_{\varphi},\chi_{\sigma}
and the mobility
m(\cdot)
in related models has been extensively studied, see, e.g.,
[
23
,
31
,
32
]
, and we observed similar behaviour for our model. For that reason, we focus the presentation of the numerical tests on the effects arising from viscoelasticity.

The main difficulty is to find a good choice of parameters. To observe an unstable growth, i.e. the development of fingers, the chemotaxis parameter
\chi_{\varphi}
has to be in the same scale as
\frac{\beta}{\epsilon}
.
Choosing
\chi_{\varphi}
too large or
\beta
too small reduces the forming of the pure phases
\varphi=\pm 1
.
On the other side, we observe a jump of the nutrient
\sigma
along the interface which is proportional to
\chi_{\sigma}^{-1}\chi_{\varphi}
, hence
\chi_{\sigma}
is chosen large compared to
\chi_{\varphi}
.
However, this can result in very large velocities if the viscosities
\eta_{1},\eta_{-1}
are not large enough.

5.2
Comparison with the fully viscous model

We now investigate the time evolution in the viscoelastic model and compare it to the fully viscous model where
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{I}
.
The parameters are chosen as in (
5.1
) and the goal is to observe an unstable growth.
In absence of initial elastic stresses, i.e.
\mathbb{B}_{0}=\mathbb{I}
, any changes in the left Cauchy–Green tensor
\mathbb{B}
are induced by the velocity field
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}
, see (
4.48
). As the viscosities
\eta_{1},\eta_{-1}
are chosen very large, we can expect small velocities and hence
\mathbb{B}\approx\mathbb{I}
, such that the elastic stress tensor is approximately zero, i.e.
\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\mathbb{B})=\kappa(\mathbb{B}-\mathbb{I})\approx 0
.
Therefore, we expect that the qualitative behaviour of both models is very similar, which can be observed in Figure
5
. Here, we show the numerical solutions for both models at time
t=2
. In the first row from left to right, we show the order parameter
\varphi
, the nutrient
\sigma
, the velocity magnitude
\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert
and the final mesh for the fully viscous model. In the second row from left to right, the order parameter
\varphi
, the nutrient
\sigma
, the velocity magnitude
\left\lvert{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}\right\rvert
and the magnitude of the elastic stress tensor
\left\lvert{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\mathbb{B})}\right\rvert
of the viscoelastic model are visualized.
Indeed, the qualitative behaviour for both models is very similar, as
\left\lvert{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\mathbb{B})}\right\rvert\approx 0
is close to machine precision for the viscoelastic model.
In both cases, the tumour has developed fingers showing towards regions with higher concentration of the nutrient which can be interpreted as the chemotaxis effect, i.e. the cell movement in response to an extracellular chemical gradient. This behaviour has also been observed for other models
[
23
,
31
,
32
]
.
After that, in Figure
6
, we show the time evolution of the tumour for the viscoelastic model at the times
t\in\{0.5,1,1.5,2\}
.

. In the first three columns,

.

5.3
Influence of the viscosity

In the next experiment, we illustrate how the choice of the viscosity function
\eta(\cdot)
can affect the elastic stress tensor and hence the evolution of the tumour. We increase the proliferation rate, i.e.
\mathcal{P}=5
, and decrease the chemotactic sensitivity, i.e.
\chi_{\varphi}=7.5
. Moreover, we increase the relaxation times such that
\frac{\tau_{1}}{\kappa}=\frac{\tau_{-1}}{\kappa}=100
and choose the viscosities
\eta_{1}=2000
and
\eta_{-1}\in\{500,1000,1500\}
.
The numerical solutions
\varphi
(first row) and
\left\lvert{\mathbb{T}_{\mathrm{el}}(\mathbb{B})}\right\rvert
(second row) at time
t=1.6
are visualized in Figure
7
for the cases
\eta_{-1}\in\{500,1000,1500\}
. From left to right, the elastic stresses decrease and the size of the tumours increase with increasing viscosity
\eta_{-1}
. However, note that the elasticity parameter
\kappa=10^{4}
is chosen very large in order to see the influence of the elastic stresses.

.

5.4
Mechanical stresses generated by growth

Now we consider a variant of the model with an additional source term in the equation of
\mathbb{B}
like in (
2.71
). For this reason we replace (
4.48
) by

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\Big(\frac{\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n-1}}{\Delta t}+\frac{\kappa}{\tau(\varphi_{h}^{n-1})}(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-\mathbb{I})\Big):\mathbb{C}_{h}\Big]-2\nabla\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n}:(\mathbb{C}_{h}\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})+\alpha\nabla\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}:\nabla\mathbb{C}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{I}_{h}\Big[\gamma(\varphi_{h}^{n-1},\sigma_{h}^{n-1})\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}:\mathbb{C}_{h}\Big]-\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}:((\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}_{h}^{n-1}\cdot\nabla)\mathbb{C}_{h})\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(5.5)

where
\gamma(\varphi,\sigma)=\mathcal{G}\sigma h(\varphi)
with a constant
\mathcal{G}\geq 0
.

For the first numerical test, the parameters are chosen as in (
5.1
) but with

\mathcal{P}=5
,
\chi_{\varphi}=7.5
,
\eta_{1}=2000
,
\eta_{-1}=1500
,
\frac{\tau_{1}}{\kappa}=\frac{\tau_{-1}}{\kappa}=0.01
and
\mathcal{G}\in\{0,0.1,0.5,1\}
.
In Figure
8
, we visualize the tumour (upper row) and the magnitude of the elastic stress tensor (lower row) where
\mathcal{G}\in\{0,0.1,0.2,0.5\}
from left to right at time
t=2.25
. From left to right, the size of the tumours decrease as the elastic stresses become larger with increasing
\mathcal{G}
. Moreover, we observe that the elastic stresses are particularly large in the fingers of the tumour.

. First row:

at time

.

Next, we present the influence of the relaxation times
\tau_{-1},\tau_{1}
on the growth behaviour in presence of source terms for
\mathbb{B}
.
On one side, we expect the elastic stresses to vanish if the relaxation time is small enough. On the other side, large elastic stresses can build up if the relaxation time is large, which then reduces the proliferation effect.
Therefore, the parameters are chosen as in (
5.1
) but with

\mathcal{P}=5
,
\chi_{\varphi}=7.5
,
\mathcal{G}=0.2
and with
\frac{\tau_{1}}{\kappa}=\frac{\tau_{-1}}{\kappa}\in\{10^{-4},10^{-2},1\}
.

, we visualize the tumour (upper row) and the magnitude of the elastic stress tensor (lower row) at time
t=2.4
, where
\frac{\tau_{1}}{\kappa}=\frac{\tau_{-1}}{\kappa}\in\{10^{-4},10^{-2},1\}
from left to right. Here, no elastic stresses occur if the relaxation time is very small, and the elastic stresses can become very large if the relaxation time is large.

.

5.5
Numerical results for a phase-dependent elastic energy density

In the following, we want to illustrate the impact of a phase-dependent elastic energy density on the evolution of the tumour. In particular, we now consider the phase-dependent elasticity parameter function
\kappa(\varphi)=\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa_{1}(1+\varphi)+\tfrac{1}{2}\kappa_{-1}(1-\varphi)
, which leads to the elastic energy density
W(\varphi,\mathbb{B})=\frac{1}{2}\kappa(\varphi)\trace(\mathbb{B}-\ln\mathbb{B})
.
Hence, we adapt the system of equations (
4.48a
)–(
4.48
), (
5.4
) as follows.
First, we replace
\kappa
with
\kappa(\varphi_{h}^{n})
in (
4.48
), (
5.4
) and in the source term
\Gamma_{\varphi}
.
To be consistent with (
1.1b
), we add the term
+\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{4}(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{-1})\mathcal{I}_{h}\left[\trace(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-\ln\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\varrho_{h}\right]\,{\mathrm{d}}x
to the right-hand side of (
4.48b
).
Lastly, due to (
2.43
), we include the term
-\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{4}(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{-1})\varphi_{h}^{n-1}\nabla\trace(\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n}-\ln\mathbb{B}_{h}^{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathrm{w}}_{h}\,{\mathrm{d}}x
on the right-hand side of (
4.48
).

The goal is now to study the growth behaviour of the tumour. In particular, it is of main interest whether the chemotactic development of fingers is intensified, weakened or completely changed.
Now, as the term
W_{,\varphi}=\frac{1}{4}(\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{-1})\trace(\mathbb{B}-\ln\mathbb{B})
enters the equation for the chemical potential
\mu
, we vary the elasticity parameters
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{-1}
and we make sure that
\trace(\mathbb{B}-\ln\mathbb{B})
changes near the tumour region.
For these reasons, the parameters in the following experiments are chosen as in (
5.1
) but with
\chi_{\varphi}=7.5
,
\mathcal{G}=10
,
\frac{\tau_{1}}{\kappa_{1}}=10,\frac{\tau_{-1}}{\kappa_{-1}}=0.1
and with varying
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{-1}
.

First, we show the time evolution of the tumour with matched elasticity parameters
\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{-1}=1
at times
t\in\{1,1.5,2.3\}
and the magnitude of the elastic stress tensor at the final time. Here, the tumour growths and develops four thick fingers.

. In comparison to the case with matched elasticity parameters, we observe that the invasive growth of the tumour needs more time when
\kappa_{-1}
is large. In addition, the shapes of the tumours are more elongated and the development of fingers is barely recognizable.
